Module 0: Formula Sheets & Core Cheatsheet

Quick-lookup exam cheat sheets covering current equations, scaling models, sheet resistance delays, and FSM parameters.

💡 মনে রাখবে | Cheatsheet Shorthand
  • পরীক্ষার আগে রিভিশন: পরীক্ষার ঠিক আগের রাতে এই শিটটি দেখে গেলে সূত্র এবং ডাইমেনশন স্কেলিং প্যারামিটার সহজে মনে রাখা যাবে।
  • সমীকরণ প্রয়োগ: গাণিতিক হিসাবগুলোতে ল্যাম্বডা (\(\lambda\)) এবং শিট রেজিস্ট্যান্স (\(R_s\))-এর ইউনিটগুলো সতর্কতার সাথে ব্যবহার করবে।

VLSI Formula Cheatsheet & Scaling Reference

1. MOSFET Current and Device Equations:

Region / Parameter Condition Equation
Cutoff Region \(V_{GS} < V_{th}\) \(I_D = 0\)
Linear (Triode) \(V_{GS} \ge V_{th}\), \(V_{DS} < V_{GS} - V_{th}\) \(I_D = \beta \left[ (V_{GS} - V_{th})V_{DS} - \frac{V_{DS}^2}{2} \right]\)
Saturation Region \(V_{GS} \ge V_{th}\), \(V_{DS} \ge V_{GS} - V_{th}\) \(I_D = \frac{\beta}{2}(V_{GS} - V_{th})^2\)
Channel Modulation Saturation with modulation \(I_D = \frac{\beta}{2}(V_{GS} - V_{th})^2 (1 + \lambda V_{DS})\)

2. Scaling Factors Quick Lookup Reference:

Parameter Constant Field Scaling Constant Voltage Scaling
Dimensions (\(W, L, t_{ox}\)) \(1/\beta\) \(1/\beta\)
Voltages (\(V_{DD}, V_{GS}, V_{DS}\)) \(1/\beta\) \(1\) (Unchanged)
Electric Field (\(E\)) \(1\) (Unchanged) \(\beta\) (Increases danger)
Gate Capacitance (\(C_g\)) \(1/\beta\) \(1/\beta\)
Drain Current (\(I_{D}\)) \(1/\beta\) \(\beta\) (Increases)
Power Dissipation (\(P\)) \(1/\beta^2\) \(\beta\) (Increases)
Power Density \(1\) (Unchanged) \(\beta^3\) (Extremely high heat)

3. Sheet Resistance & Delays Cheatsheet:

  • Resistance of a path: \(R = R_s \cdot \left(\frac{\text{Length}}{\text{Width}}\right)\) where \(R_s\) is sheet resistance.
  • Delay Unit \(\tau\): \(1\tau = R_s \times C_g\) (\(\approx 0.1 - 0.3 \text{ ns}\) for NMOS).
  • CMOS Inverter Delays: Rise-time \(\tau_r \approx 2.5 \times \tau_f\) unless \(W_p \approx 2.5 W_n\) is sized to balance mobilities.
  • Super Buffer Stages count: \(N = \ln(C_L / C_g)\). Optimal staging delay: \(t_d = N \cdot e \cdot \tau\).
  • Black's Equation (Reliability): \(\text{MTF} = \frac{A}{J^n} \exp\left( \frac{E_a}{k T} \right)\).

4. FSM Design Models:

  • Moore Model: Output is purely state-driven: \(\text{Out} = f(\text{State})\).
  • Mealy Model: Output is state and input-driven: \(\text{Out} = f(\text{State}, \text{Inputs})\).

Module 1: Introduction & MOS Devices

VLSI design flow, differences from embedded systems, Moore's Law, and MOSFET physics (Enhancement & Depletion).

💡 মনে রাখবে | MOS Devices Tips & Tricks
  • Enhancement mode vs Depletion mode: Enhancement MOSFET-এ গেট ভোল্টেজ ছাড়া কারেন্ট চলে না (\(V_{GS} = 0 \implies I_D = 0\))। Depletion MOSFET-এ স্বাভাবিকভাবে চ্যানেল অন থাকে, তাই \(V_{GS} = 0\) হলেও কারেন্ট চলে (\(I_D = I_{DSS}\))।
  • Threshold Voltage (\(V_{th}\)): NMOS-এর জন্য \(V_{th} > 0\) (ইতিবাচক) এবং PMOS-এর জন্য \(V_{th} < 0\) (নেতিবাচক)।
  • Operating Regions Shortcut:
    • Cutoff: \(V_{GS} < V_{th}\) (কারেন্ট শূন্য)
    • Linear (Triode): \(V_{GS} \ge V_{th}\) এবং \(V_{DS} < V_{GS} - V_{th}\)
    • Saturation: \(V_{GS} \ge V_{th}\) এবং \(V_{DS} \ge V_{GS} - V_{th}\) (কারেন্ট ধ্রুবক)
  • Saturation Current Formula: \(I_D = \frac{\beta}{2} (V_{GS} - V_{th})^2\)
  • Body Effect (বডি ইফেক্ট): যদি source এবং body-র ভোল্টেজ আলাদা হয় (\(V_{SB} > 0\)), বডি ইফেক্টের কারণে থ্রেশহোল্ড ভোল্টেজ \(V_{th}\) বৃদ্ধি পায়। NMOS-এর জন্য বডিকে Ground এবং PMOS-এর জন্য বডিকে \(V_{DD}\)-তে কানেক্ট করে বডি ইফেক্ট কমানো হয়।
  • Channel Length Modulation (চ্যানেল দৈর্ঘ্য মডুলেশন): স্যাচুরেশন অঞ্চলে \(V_{DS}\) বাড়লে ড্রেন চ্যানেলের কার্যকর দৈর্ঘ্য কমে যায়, ফলে ড্রেন কারেন্ট সামান্য বাড়ে: \(I_D = I_{D,sat} \cdot (1 + \lambda V_{DS})\)। (\(\lambda\) হলো চ্যানেল লেন্থ মডুলেশন প্যারামিটার)।

page 1

Very-large-scale integration (VLSI) is the process of creating an IC by combining thousands of transistors into a single chip.

Ex- The microprocessor is a VLSI device.

An electronic circuit might consist of a cpu, ROM, RAM and other glue logic. VLSI lets IC makers add all of these into one chip.

Glue Logic:

Glue logic is a special form of digital circuitry that allows different types of logic chips or circuits to work together by acting as an interface between them.

Fig: Glue logic (block diagram)

Difference between VLSI and embedded System:

VLSIEmbedded System
1. VLSI deals with designing new integrated Circuits (IC).1. Embedded system is an application built using integrated circuits (Microcontrollers).
Page 2
📺 Watch Lecture VLSI Design Flow: VLSI Design Flow & Y-Chart Explained (Engineering Funda) ↗

page 2

1. For example: Designing new microcontroller like atmega16 is VLSI2. For example: Making an application using Atmega16 is embedded system.
3. VLSI- How to develop a chip3. Embedded- How to use the chips to produce an efficient system

Notes.

* The integrated circuit consist of one chip, where all the passive and active components are placed.

* SSI (Small Scale Integration)- Around a maximum of 100 transistors can fabricate on one single chip. [Year-1964] [logic-1 to 12]

* MSI (Medium Scale Integration) — [100 to 1,000 transistors] [1968] [logic gates: 13 to 99] [Counters, MUX, Decoders]

* LSI (Large Scale Integration) — [1,000 to 10,000 transistors] [1971] [logic gates: 100 to 9,999] [RAM, ROM, 4-bit/8-bit Microprocessors (Intel 4004)]

* VLSI (Very large-scale integration)-[1980][20,000 to 106 transistor] [logic-10,000 to 99,999]

* ULSI (Ultra-large-scale integration) — [1984-Present] [106 to billions of transistors] [logic gates: 100,000 and more] [Multi-core CPUs, SoCs, AI Accelerators]

VLSI Design Flow:

Page 3

page 3

Fig: VLSI Design Flow

* System specification: The first step of any design process is lay down the specifications of the system. The factors to be consider in this process include: performance, functionality, and physical dimensions.

* Architectural design: The basic architecture of the system

Page 4

page 4

is designed in this step.

*Functional design: In this step, main function units of the system are identified. This also identifies the interconnect requirements between the units.

* logic Design: In this step the control flow, word widths, register allocation, arithmetic operations, and logic operations of the design that represent the functional design are derived and tested.

* Circuit Design: The purpose of circuit design is to develop a circuit representation based on the logic design.

* physical design: In this step the circuit representation is converted into a geometric representation.

* Fabrication: After layout and verification, the design is ready for fabrication.

* Packaging and Testing: Finally, the wafer is fabricated and diced into individual chips in a fabrication facility.

# Advantage of VLSI:

  1. Reduced size for circuits
  2. Increased cost-effectiveness for devices.
Page 5

page 5

  1. Improved performance in terms of the operating speed of circuits.
  2. Requires less power than discrete components.
  3. Higher device reliability.
  4. Requires less space and promotes miniaturization.

Application of VLSI

  1. Defense
  2. Aerospace
  3. Wireless and wireline communication
  4. Consumer electronics
  5. Bio electronics
  6. Medical electronics
  7. Electronic design optimization

# Integration and Moore's law:

The number of transistors in a dense integrated circuit will be doubled about every two years.

Integration levels

SSI : 10 GATES
MSI: 1000 GATES
LSI : 10,000 GATES
VLSI: >10K GATES

# Propagation delay:

Propagation delay typically refers to the rise time or fall time in logic gates.

# Reasons for CMOS being the dominant technology:

  1. These gates are very simple
  2. Input impedance is high
  3. CMOS logic uses less power
  4. Power dissipation is negligible
  5. Compact
  6. Designing is very well
  7. Mechanically robust
  8. Fan out is high TTL compatibility
  9. Stability of temperature
  10. Noise immunity is good.

# Fan Out:

Fan out is the number of gate inputs driven by the output of another single logic gate.

TTL (Transistor-Transistor Logic)

The transistor-transistor logic (TTL) is a logic family made up of BJTs (Bipolar Junction transistor). As the name

Page 8

page 8

suggests, the transistor perform two function like logic as well as amplifying.

CMOS (Complementary Metal oxide semiconductor)

CMOS transistors are based on metal-oxide semiconductor field-effect transistor technology. MOSFETs serve as switches or amplifiers that control the amount of electricity flowing between source and drain terminals, based on the amount of applied voltage.

Compare between TTL and CMOS Technology:

  1. TTL circuits utilize BJTs while CMOS circuits utilize FETs.
  2. CMOS allows a much higher density of logic function in a single chip compared to TTL.
  3. TTL circuits consumes more power compared to CMOS circuit at rest.
  4. CMOS chips are a lot more susceptible to static discharge compared to TTL chips.
  5. CMOS has high fan-out capacity but TTL has lower fan-out capacity.
Page 9

page 9

  1. CMOS has slow propagation delays and TTL has fast propagation delays.

# MOS Device #

A Metal-Oxide-Semiconductor Field-Effect Transistor (MOSFET) is a four-terminal voltage-controlled transistor with an insulated gate dielectric (SiO₂), operating in either Enhancement Mode or Depletion Mode.

* Type of MOSFET

# Operation of D-MOSFET

1. Depletion mode

Fig: Depletion mode circuit — battery connected D to S through gate, n-channel over P-substrate cross-section
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ TECHNICAL SCHEMATIC SPECIFICATION                        │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Block : Depletion mode circuit                           │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ • battery connected D to S through gate, n-channel over  │
│   P-substrate cross-section                              │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

The resistance of the channel is increase the greater the negative voltage on the gate, the lesser is the current from source to drain.

Thus by changing the negative voltage on the gate, we can vary the resistance of the n-channel and hence the

Page 11
📺 Watch Lecture D-MOSFET Characteristics: Drain & Transfer Characteristics of D-MOSFET (Neso Academy) ↗

page 11

current from source to drain.

* With negative gate depends upon depleting the channel of free electron the negative-gate operation is called depletion mode.

2. Enhancement mode

Fig: Enhancement mode — n-channel D-MOSFET cross section, S-G-D with n regions in P substrate; also small "+" charge layer diagram on right
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ TECHNICAL SCHEMATIC SPECIFICATION                        │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Block : Enhancement mode                                 │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ • n-channel D-MOSFET cross section, S-G-D with n regions │
│   in P substrate                                         │
│ • also small "+" charge layer diagram on right           │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

show enhancement-mode operation of n-channel D-MOSFET. Again, the gate acts like a capacitor. Since, the gate is positive, it induce negative charges in the channel. These negative charge are the free electron drawn into the channel. Because these free electron are added to those already in channel, the total number of free electron in the channel in increase the conductivity of the channel. The greater the positive voltage on the gate, greater the conduction from source to drain.

# D-MOSFET Transfer characteristic

Fig: D-MOSFET transconductance curve (Id vs \(V_{GS}\)), parabola through origin at \(V_{GS(\text{th})}\) on negative side, through \(I_{DSS}\) at \(V_{GS} = 0\), continuing to positive \(V_{GS}\) region
Page 12
📺 Watch Lecture Enhancement MOSFET: Construction & Working of Enhancement-Type MOSFET (Neso Academy) ↗

page 12

* The transconductance equation.

\[I_D = I_{DSS} \left[ 1 - \frac{V_{GS}}{V_{GS(\text{off})}} \right]^2\]

# Operation of E-MOSFET

Fig: E-MOSFET cross-sections — (two variants) showing S-G-D terminals over n regions in substrate
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ TECHNICAL SCHEMATIC SPECIFICATION                        │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Block : E-MOSFET cross-sections                          │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ • (two variants) showing S-G-D terminals over n regions  │
│   in substrate                                           │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

When \(V_{GS} = 0\), there is no channel connecting the source and drain.

When gate is made positive (\(V_{GS}\) is positive) as shown, it attracts free electron into the p region. The free electron combine with the hole next to the SiO2 layer. If \(V_{GS}\) is positive enough, all the hole touching the SiO2 layer are filled and free electron begin to flow from the source to drain. The effect is the same as creating a thin-n channel. Thus the E-MOSFET is turned on and drain current Id starts flowing from the source to drain.

Page 13
📺 Watch Lecture MOSFET Drain Characteristics: Drain Characteristics of Enhancement MOSFET (Neso Academy) ↗

page 13

The minimum value of \(V_{GS}\) that turn the E-MOSFET ON is called threshold voltage \(V_{GS(\text{th})}\).

# CMOS Inverter #

The inverter:

Figure-1 shows the schematic and symbol for cmos inverter or NOT gate using one nmos transistor and one pmos transistor. The bar at the top indicates \(V_{DD}\) and the triangle at the bottom indicates GND. When the input A is 0, the nmos transistor is off and pmos transistor is on. Thus, the output Y is pulled up to 1 because it is connected to \(V_{DD}\) but not to GND. Conversely, when A in 1, the nmos is ON, the pmos is off and Y is pulled down to 0. This is

Fig: CMOS inverter circuit — \(V_{DD}\) at top, pmos above, nmos below, A as common gate input, Y as output between them, GND at bottom

# K-Input CMOS NAND GATE

K-input NAND gates are constructed using K series nmos and K-parallel pmos transistors.

A 3-input NAND gate is shown in figure. When any of the input are 0, the output is pulled high through the parallel pmos transistors. When all of the inputs are 1,

Page 17

page 17

the output is pulled low through the series nmos transistors.

# Pull up and Pull Down networks #

The inverter and NAND gates are examples of static cmos logic gates, also called complementary cmos gates.

Page 18

page 18

A static cmos gate has an n-mos pull-down network to connect the output to GND(0) and p-mos pull-up network to connect the output to 1 (\(V_{DD}\)). The networks are arranged such that one is ON and the other OFF for any input pattern.

Fig: General logic gate using pull-up and pull-down networks

# Series and Parallel behavior of nmos and pmos #

Two or more transistors in series are ON only if all the series transistors are ON.

Page 19

page 19

Fig: nmos in series (gates a, b) — truth table of series combination states
Series nmos (gate1=a, gate2=b) switch truth table:
 a=0,b=0 → OFF | a=0,b=1 → OFF | a=1,b=0 → OFF | a=1,b=1 → ON
Fig: pmos in series (gates a, b) — truth table
Series pmos (gate1=a, gate2=b):
 a=0,b=0 → ON | a=0,b=1 → OFF | a=1,b=0 → OFF | a=1,b=1 → OFF

Series and parallel behavior of nmos and pmos:

Two or more transistors in parallel are ON if any one of the parallel transistors are ON.

Fig: nmos in parallel (gates a,b) truth table
a=0,b=0 → OFF | a=0,b=1 → ON | a=1,b=0 → ON | a=1,b=1 → ON
Fig: pmos in parallel (gates a,b) truth table
a=0,b=0 → ON | a=0,b=1 → ON | a=1,b=0 → ON | a=1,b=1 → OFF
Page 20

page 20

# Possible stages of CMOS #

* The 1 and 0 levels have been encountered with inverter and NAND gates, where either the pull-up or pull-down is off and the other structure is ON.

* When both pull-up and pull-down are off, the high-impedance or floating Z output state results. This is of importance in multiplexers, memory elements and tristate bus drivers.

* The crowbarred (or contention) X level exists when both pull-up and pull-down are simultaneously turned on. Contention between the two networks results in an indeterminate output level and dissipates static power. Usually an unwanted condition.

pull-up OFFpull-up ON
pull-down OFFZ1
pull-down ON0crowbarred (X)
Page 21

page 21

# The NOR Gate

A 2-input NOR gate is shown in figure. The nmos transistor are in parallel to pull the output low when either input is high. The pmos transistors are in series to pull the output high when both inputs are low, as indicated in Table-4. The output is never crowbarred or left floating.

Fig: 2 input gate schematic — NOR gate: \(V_{DD}\), two pmos in series (pull-up), two nmos in parallel (pull-down), inputs A,B, output Y, GND

# Compound Gates Using CMOS #

* AND → pMOS – parallel
       NMOS – series

* OR → pMOS – series
       NMOS – parallel

# Ex- \(Y = \overline{(A \cdot B) + (C \cdot D)}\) (complemented, i.e. NOR of two ANDs)

Fig: CMOS compound gate for function \(Y = \overline{(A \cdot B) + (C \cdot D)}\)
Pull-down network: series pair (A,B) in parallel with series pair (C,D) → nmos
Pull-up network: parallel pair (A,B) in series with parallel pair (C,D) → pmos
Logic-gate equivalent: AND(A,B) and AND(C,D) feeding a NOR gate → Y

page 23

AND → pMOS – parallel, NMOS – series

OR → pMOS – series, NMOS – Parallel

Basic structure shown at right: VDD → Pull-up pMOS network → o/p ← Pull-down nMOS network → GND

# Pass Transistor and Transmission logic #

Pass Transistors:

When nmos and pmos transistor is used alone, as an imperfect switch we call them as pass transistors.

* nmos:-

An nmos transistor is an almost perfect switch while

Page 30

page 30

passing a '0' and thus we say that it passes a strong zero. However, it is an imperfect switch while passing a 1. We say it passes a weak or degraded 1.

Fig: nmos pass switch behavior — (a) g=0: switch open s—d; g=1: switch closed s—d. (b) truth table: input g=1: 0 input→strong 0 output; 1 input→degraded 1 output
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ TECHNICAL SCHEMATIC SPECIFICATION                        │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Block : nmos pass switch behavior                        │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ • (a) g=0: switch open s—d                               │
│ • g=1: switch closed s—d. (b) truth table: input g=1: 0  │
│   input→strong 0 output                                  │
│ • 1 input→degraded 1 output                              │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

* PMOS:- The pmos show just the opposite behavior.

Fig: pmos pass switch behavior — g=0: closed; g=1: open. Truth table: input g=0: 0 input→degraded 0 output; g=0: 1 input→strong 1 output
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ TECHNICAL SCHEMATIC SPECIFICATION                        │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Block : pmos pass switch behavior                        │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ • g=0: closed                                            │
│ • g=1: open. Truth table: input g=0: 0 input→degraded 0  │
│   output                                                 │
│ • g=0: 1 input→strong 1 output                           │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

# Transmission Logic:

By combining an nmos and pmos transistor in parallel we obtain a switch that turn on when a 1 is applied to g (fig-b) in which 0s and 1s are both passed in an acceptable fashion (fig-c). We term this a transmission gate or pass gate.

Fig: Transmission gate symbol — nmos and pmos in parallel between terminals a and b, gate \(g\) on nMOS and \(\overline{g}\) on pMOS
Page 31

page 31

Fig: Transmission gate truth table (a,b terminals with g,~g control)
g=0,g̅=1: switch open (a—o o—b) [High-Impedance Z]
g=1,g̅=0: switch closed (a—o—o—b) [Conducting]
Operation:
  • g=1, g̅=0 (ON):
      input 0 → strong 0 output (via NMOS)
      input 1 → strong 1 output (via PMOS)
  • g=0, g̅=1 (OFF):
      both NMOS & PMOS are OFF → output isolated (High-Z)

symbol of transmission gate:-

Fig: Transmission gate — three symbol variants (a—b with g/~g control): crossed-triangle style, circled-X style, and buffer/arrow style
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ TECHNICAL SCHEMATIC SPECIFICATION                        │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Block : Transmission gate                                │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ • three symbol variants (a—b with g/~g control):         │
│   crossed-triangle style, circled-X style, and           │
│   buffer/arrow style                                     │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

# Fabrication #

Fabrication refers to the process of creating semiconductor devices, such as integr...(ated circuits)

The fabrication process involves several key steps:

1. photolithography

A light-sensitive material (photoresist) is applied to the wafer, and ultraviolet light is applied to the wafer used to transfer the circuit pattern from a photomask onto the wafer.

Page 32

page 32

2. Etching:

After photolithography, the unwanted material is removed by chemical or plasma etching, creating the desired patterns on the wafer.

3. Doping:

Impurities (dopants) are introduced to modify the electrical properties of specific regions on the silicon wafer.

4. Deposition:

Layers of materials such as metals or insulators are deposited onto the wafer to form different components such as transistor, resistors and capacitors.

5. Oxidation:

The wafer is exposed to oxygen to form an oxide layer, which serves as an insulator or protective layer.

6. planarization:

Chemical mechanical polishing is used to smoothen the surface of the wafer between fabrication steps.

7. Metallization

Thin layer of metal are deposited to create electrical connections between different parts of the IC.

Page 33

page 33

# nMOS Fabrication:-

step 1: Processing is done on thin wafer cut from a single crystal of Si of high purity.

Fig: p-substrate wafer (blank rectangle labeled "P-substrate, wafer")

# The pass Transistor #

A pass transistor is a MOSFET that is used to control the passage of a signal between nodes, rather than switching a circuit on or off. It functions as a switch that pass logic level (either '1' or '0') from the input to the output when the transistor is turned on.

Fig: Pass transistor chain — \(V_{DD}\) through three nmos switches gated by A, B, C in series to node X
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ TECHNICAL SCHEMATIC SPECIFICATION                        │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Block : Pass transistor chain                            │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ • VDD through three nmos switches gated by A, B, C in    │
│   series to node X                                       │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

∴ \(V_{DD}\) = input

Page 46
📺 Watch Lecture Body Effect: Body Effect in MOSFET & Threshold Voltage Shift (Engineering Funda) ↗

page 46

\(\therefore \text{Output, } X = V_{DD} - V_t \quad (\text{if logic } A \cdot B \cdot C = 1)\)

Where \(V_t = \text{Threshold voltage}\)

Note important:

Fig: Pass transistor control — input A → nmos gated by control terminal C=1 [on→1, off→0], threshold voltage \(V_{\text{th}}\), output y
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ TECHNICAL SCHEMATIC SPECIFICATION                        │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Block : Pass transistor control                          │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ • input A → nmos gated by control terminal C=1 [on→1,    │
│   off→0], threshold voltage Vth, output y                │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  1. When c=1, the nMOS transistor is on.
  2. Thus, when i/p we give at A, we get it at the output y.

Why do you need pass transistor logic?

  1. It is reduced the number of transistor for implement logic (AND, OR, etc).
  2. It reduce the required space for chips.

Main problem in pass transistor logic

Fig: Two cascaded pass transistors — (C=1 each), \(V_{\text{in}}\) → \(V_{\text{th}}\) drop → \(V_{\text{out1}}\) → \(V_{\text{th}}\) drop → \(V_{\text{out2}}\), threshold voltage loss shown
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ TECHNICAL SCHEMATIC SPECIFICATION                        │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Block : Two cascaded pass transistors                    │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ • (C=1 each), Vin → Vth drop → Vout1 → Vth drop → Vout2, │
│   threshold voltage loss shown                           │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\[\therefore V_{\text{out1}} = V_{\text{in}} - V_{\text{th}}\]
\[V_{\text{out2}} = V_{\text{in}} - V_{\text{th}} - V_{\text{th}} = V_{\text{in}} - 2V_{\text{th}}\]
Page 47
📺 Watch Lecture Channel Length Modulation: Channel Length Modulation in MOSFET (Engineering Funda) ↗

page 47

When input voltage is passing through pass transistor that is reduced.

# nMOS inverter #

An NMOS inverter is a basic digital circuit used in VLSI design that inverts the input signal using an nmos transistor and a pull-up resistor.

Note: Ideal inverter

\(A \longrightarrow \text{NOT} \longrightarrow Y = \overline{A}\)

A\(Y = \overline{A}\)
01
10
Page 48
📺 Watch Lecture Channel Length Modulation: Channel Length Modulation in MOSFET (Engineering Funda) ↗

page 48

Voltage transfer characteristic of ideal inverter

* circuit operation, consider:
logic (1) means high voltage \(V_{DD}\)
logic (0) means low voltage ground (0V)

Fig: Ideal inverter VTC (\(V_{\text{out}}\) vs \(V_{\text{in}}\)) — step function: \(V_{\text{out}}\)=\(V_{DD}\) for \(V_{\text{in}}\) in logic(0) range [0,\(V_{DD}\)/2), \(V_{\text{out}}\)=0 for \(V_{\text{in}}\) in logic(1) range (\(V_{DD}\)/2,\(V_{DD}\)]
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ TECHNICAL SCHEMATIC SPECIFICATION                        │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Block : Ideal inverter VTC (Vout vs Vin)                 │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ • step function: Vout=VDD for Vin in logic(0) range      │
│   [0,VDD/2), Vout=0 for Vin in logic(1) range            │
│   (VDD/2,VDD]                                            │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\[\therefore 0 \le V_{\text{in}} \le V_{\text{th}} \quad (\text{Input logic } 0)\]
\[V_{\text{th}} \le V_{\text{in}} \le V_{DD} \quad (\text{Input logic } 1)\]

Circuit of nmos inverter

Fig: nmos inverter circuit — \(V_{DD}\) → depletion-mode load transistor → \(V_{\text{out}}\) node → enhancement-mode nmos (gate=\(V_{\text{in}}\)) → GND; \(C_{\text{out}}\) capacitance at output shown

📺 Watch Lecture CMOS Inverter: CMOS Inverter Circuit & Working Explained (Engineering Funda) ↗

page 49

* Due to capacitance of gate, no gate current is flowing. Hence

\[I_L = I_D\]

If \(V_{\text{in}}\) is less, ID is minimum

\[V_{\text{out}} = V_{DD} - I_L \times R_{\text{load}}\]
\[\approx V_{DD}\]

* If we further increase \(V_{\text{in}}\), it will form inversion layer. So ID current will increase,

\[V_{\text{out}} = V_{DD} - I_L \times R_{\text{load}}\]
\[V_{\text{out}} \approx 0\text{ V}\]

Marked: VIL, Vth, VIH, Vinv on x-axis; VOH (approx VDD) and VOL (set by ratio Zpu / Zpd) on y-axis; regions labeled "input logic '0'" and "input logic '1'"

circuit diagram of nmos:

Fig: nmos inverter — \(V_{DD}\) → depletion-mode pull-up transistor → \(V_{\text{out}}\) → enhancement-mode nmos (gate=\(V_{\text{in}}\)) → GND

Note:

* For the depletion mode transistor, it is always on. if \(V_{GS}\) = 0

* In this configuration the depletion mode device is called the pull-up (p.u) and the enhancement mode device the pull-down (p.d) transistor

* Pull-up to pull-down resistance ratio \(= Z_{p.u} / Z_{p.d}\)

Where \(Z = \text{Length} / \text{Width} = L / W\)

During transition, the slope of the transfer char. determines the gain:

\[\text{Gain} = \frac{\partial V_{\text{out}}}{\partial V_{\text{in}}}\]

Determination of pull-up to pull-down ratio (\(Z_{pu}/Z_{pd}\)) for an nmos inverter driven by another nmos inverter.

Fig: Two cascaded nmos inverters — inverter1 (input A/\(V_{\text{in1}}\)) → node \(V_{\text{in}}\)* → inverter2 → \(V_{\text{out}}\)
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ TECHNICAL SCHEMATIC SPECIFICATION                        │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Block : Two cascaded nmos inverters                      │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ • inverter1 (input A/Vin1) → node Vin* → inverter2 →     │
│   Vout                                                   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\[V_{\text{in}} = V_{\text{out}} = V_{\text{inv}}\]

Fig: nmos inverter driven directly by another inverter.

We set \(V_{\text{inv}} = 0.5\,V_{DD}\). At this point both transistor are in saturation and.

\[I_{DS} = \frac{K}{2} \left(\frac{W}{L}\right) (V_{GS} - V_t)^2 \qquad \text{--- (1)}\]

In the depletion mode,

\[I_{DS} = \frac{K}{2} \left(\frac{W_{p.u}}{L_{p.u}}\right) (-V_{td})^2 \quad (\text{since } V_{GS} = 0) \qquad \text{--- (2)}\]

In the enhancement mode,

\[I_{DS} = \frac{K}{2} \left(\frac{W_{p.d}}{L_{p.d}}\right) (V_{\text{inv}} - V_t)^2 \quad (\text{since } V_{GS} = V_{\text{inv}})\]
Page 53

page 53

Equating we have,

\[\left(\frac{W_{p.d}}{L_{p.d}}\right)(V_{\text{inv}} - V_t)^2 = \left(\frac{W_{p.u}}{L_{p.u}}\right)(-V_{td})^2\]
\[\implies \frac{1}{Z_{p.d}}(V_{\text{inv}} - V_t)^2 = \frac{1}{Z_{p.u}}(-V_{td})^2\]
\[\implies \frac{(V_{\text{inv}} - V_t)^2}{(-V_{td})^2} = \frac{Z_{p.d}}{Z_{p.u}}\]
\[\implies \frac{-V_{td}}{V_{\text{inv}} - V_t} = \sqrt{\frac{Z_{p.u}}{Z_{p.d}}}\]
\[\implies V_{\text{inv}} - V_t = \frac{-V_{td}}{\sqrt{Z_{p.u} / Z_{p.d}}}\]
\[\implies V_{\text{inv}} = V_t - \frac{V_{td}}{\sqrt{Z_{p.u} / Z_{p.d}}} \qquad \text{--- (3)}\]

Now, we can substitute typical value as follows:

\[V_t = 0.2\,V_{DD} \,;\quad V_{td} = -0.6\,V_{DD}\]
\[V_{\text{inv}} = 0.5\,V_{DD}\]

From eqn (3):

\[0.5 = 0.2 + \frac{0.6}{\sqrt{Z_{p.u} / Z_{p.d}}}\]
\[\implies \sqrt{\frac{Z_{p.u}}{Z_{p.d}}} = 2\]
Page 54

page 54

\[\implies \frac{Z_{p.u}}{Z_{p.d}} = \frac{4}{1}\]

For an inverter directly by an inverter.

Pull-up to pull-down ratio for an nmos inverter driven through one or more pass transistors:

Fig: pull-up to pull-down ratios for inverting logic coupled by pass transistors — inverter1 (input A/\(V_{\text{in1}}\)) → node B through nmos pass transistor gated by \(V_{DD}\) → node C (=\(V_{DD} - V_{tp}\)) → inverter2 → \(V_{\text{out2}}\)
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ TECHNICAL SCHEMATIC SPECIFICATION                        │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Block : pull-up to pull-down ratios for inverting logic  │
│         coupled by pass transistors                      │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ • inverter1 (input A/Vin1) → node B through nmos pass    │
│   transistor gated by VDD → node C (=VDD−Vtp) →          │
│   inverter2 → Vout2                                      │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Now consider the arrangement of figure in which the input to inverter2 from the output of inverter1 but passes through one or more nmos transistors used as switches in series (called pass transistor).

Fig: (a) inverter1 with input=\(V_{DD}\) — depletion mode T1 with current I1, enhancement mode transistor with output \(V_{\text{out1}}\)
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ TECHNICAL SCHEMATIC SPECIFICATION                        │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Block : (a) inverter1 with input=VDD                     │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ • depletion mode T1 with current I1, enhancement mode    │
│   transistor with output Vout1                           │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Fig: (b) inverter2 with input=\(V_{DD} - V_{tp}\) — depletion mode T1 with current I2, enhancement mode transistor with output \(V_{\text{out2}}\)
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ TECHNICAL SCHEMATIC SPECIFICATION                        │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Block : (b) inverter2 with input=VDD−Vtp                 │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ • depletion mode T1 with current I2, enhancement mode    │
│   transistor with output Vout2                           │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Page 55

page 55

The input voltage to inverter2 is

\[V_{\text{in2}} = V_{DD} - V_{tp} \quad (\text{where } V_{tp} = \text{threshold voltage for a pass transistor})\]

For the p.d transistor,

\[I_{DS} = K \left(\frac{W_{p.d.1}}{L_{p.d.1}}\right) \left[(V_{DD} - V_t)\cdot V_{DS1} - \frac{V_{DS1}^2}{2}\right] \qquad \text{--- (1)}\]
\[\therefore R_1 = \frac{V_{DS1}}{I_{DS}}\]
\[= \frac{1}{K}\left(\frac{L_{p.d.1}}{W_{p.d.1}}\right)\left[\frac{1}{V_{DD} - V_t - V_{DS1}/2}\right]\]
\[= \frac{1}{K} \cdot Z_{p.d.1} \cdot \left[\frac{1}{V_{DD} - V_t}\right] \qquad \text{--- (2)}\]

Here, \(V_{DS1}\) = small and \(V_{DS1}/2\) may be ignored

\[Z_{p.d.1} = \frac{L_{p.d.1}}{W_{p.d.1}}\]

Now, for depletion mode p.u in saturation with \(V_{GS} = 0\)

\[I_1 = I_{\text{sb}} = K \left(\frac{W_{p.u.1}}{L_{p.u.1}}\right)\frac{(-V_{td})^2}{2} \qquad \text{--- (3)}\]

We know that,

\[V_{\text{out1}} = I_1 \cdot R_1\]
\[= \left(\frac{Z_{p.d.1}}{Z_{p.u.1}}\right) \cdot \left(\frac{1}{V_{DD} - V_t}\right) \cdot \frac{(V_{td})^2}{2} \qquad \text{--- (4)}\]
Page 56

page 56

Consider inverter2, when input = \(V_{DD} - V_{tp}\).

\[R_2 = \frac{1}{K} \cdot Z_{p.d.2} \cdot \left[\frac{1}{(V_{DD} - V_{tp}) - V_t}\right]\]
\[I_2 = K \left(\frac{1}{Z_{p.u.2}}\right) \cdot \left\{\frac{(-V_{td})^2}{2}\right\}\]

∴ The output,

\[V_{\text{out2}} = I_2 \cdot R_2\]
\[= \left(\frac{Z_{p.d.2}}{Z_{p.u.2}}\right) \cdot \left(\frac{1}{V_{DD} - V_{tp} - V_t}\right) \cdot \frac{(-V_{td})^2}{2}\]

Now, \(V_{\text{out1}}\) = \(V_{\text{out2}}\)

\[\implies I_1 R_1 = I_2 R_2\]
\[\implies \frac{Z_{p.u.2}}{Z_{p.d.2}} = \left(\frac{Z_{p.u.1}}{Z_{p.d.1}}\right) \cdot \frac{V_{DD} - V_t}{V_{DD} - V_{tp} - V_t} \qquad \text{--- (5)}\]

Taking typical values,

\[V_t = 0.2\,V_{DD}\]
\[V_{tp} = 0.3\,V_{DD}\]
\[\frac{Z_{p.u.2}}{Z_{p.d.2}} = \left(\frac{Z_{p.u.1}}{Z_{p.d.1}}\right) \cdot \left(\frac{0.8}{0.5}\right)\]

Note: \((0.8 / 0.5) = 1.6\), which gives \(1.6 \times (4/1) = 6.4/1\). Accounting for the body effect (substrate bias) increasing the pass-transistor threshold voltage \(V_{tp}\) to \(\approx 0.35V_{DD} - 0.4V_{DD}\), the ratio factor rises to approximately 2.0. Hence, by convention:

\[\frac{Z_{p.u.2}}{Z_{p.d.2}} \ge 2 \times \left(\frac{Z_{p.u.1}}{Z_{p.d.1}}\right) = 2 \times \frac{4}{1} = \frac{8}{1}\]

2024 2024)1)a [4 Marks] Explain the top-down design approach in VLSI technology. How does it facilitate efficient design and integration of complex systems? Discuss the key steps involved.
▼
Sol

1. Concept of Top-Down Design Methodology:

The Top-Down Design Approach is a structured, hierarchical VLSI design methodology in which a complex system is first specified at the highest level of abstraction (System / Behavioral Level) and then recursively partitioned into smaller, manageable sub-blocks down through intermediate levels (Architectural / RTL Level \(\to\) Logic Gate Level \(\to\) Transistor Circuit Level \(\to\) Physical Mask Layout) until leaf cells can be laid out on silicon.

2. How It Facilitates Efficient Design & Integration of Complex Systems:

  • Complexity Management (Divide-and-Conquer): Decomposes multi-million/billion-transistor SoCs into modular functional blocks (ALU, register file, memory controller, I/O) with well-defined interfaces.
  • Concurrent Team Engineering: Once block boundaries, timing budgets, and pin interfaces are frozen at the top level, multiple engineering teams can design and verify different subsystems in parallel.
  • Early Bug Detection & CAD Automation: High-level behavioral simulation and RTL verification catch architectural flaws early before expensive physical layout, while enabling automated Logic Synthesis and Place-and-Route (P&R) tools.
  • Reusability (IP Cores): Standardized leaf cells and verified RTL blocks can be reused across chip generations.

3. Key Sequential Steps Involved:

  1. System Specification: Defining functionality, performance (clock speed), power envelope, die area, and cost targets.
  2. Architectural & High-Level Design: Partitioning into datapath, control unit, memory arrays, and bus structure.
  3. RTL Coding & Functional Simulation: Writing synthesizable Verilog/VHDL code and verifying behavior via testbenches.
  4. Logic Synthesis & Gate-Level Verification: Mapping RTL into a standard-cell gate netlist and running Static Timing Analysis (STA).
  5. Physical Design & Tape-Out: Floorplanning, Placement, Clock Tree Synthesis (CTS), Routing, DRC/LVS sign-off, and GDSII mask generation.
2024 2024)1)b [5 Marks] Mention the aspects involved in VLSI technological growth over time and also explore the concept of Moore's Law and its implications for VLSI technology.
▼
Sol

1. Key Aspects of VLSI Technological Growth Over Time:

  • Evolution of Integration Scales: Progression from SSI (Small-Scale Integration, \(10\text{--}100\) transistors) \(\to\) MSI (\(100\text{--}1{,}000\)) \(\to\) LSI (\(1{,}000\text{--}100{,}000\)) \(\to\) VLSI (\(10^5\text{--}10^7\)) \(\to\) ULSI / GSI (billions of transistors per die).
  • Minimum Feature Size Scaling (\(L_{\min}\)): Photolithography wavelength reduction (UV \(\to\) DUV \(193\text{ nm}\) immersion \(\to\) EUV \(13.5\text{ nm}\)) shrank MOSFET channel length from \(10\,\mu\text{m}\) (1970s) to sub-\(3\text{ nm}\) nodes.
  • Wafer Diameter Expansion: Silicon wafers grew from \(50\text{ mm}\) (2-inch) to \(300\text{ mm}\) (12-inch), dramatically increasing dies per wafer and lowering manufacturing cost per transistor.
  • Materials & Device Architecture Innovations: Transition from NMOS to zero-static-power CMOS, Aluminum to Copper interconnects with Low-\(k\) dielectrics, High-\(k\)/Metal Gates (HKMG), and 3D device structures (Planar MOSFET \(\to\) FinFET \(\to\) Gate-All-Around Nanosheet FETs).

2. Concept of Moore's Law:

Formulated by Gordon Moore (1965/1975), Moore's Law observes that the number of transistors integrated on a single silicon chip doubles approximately every \(18\text{ to }24\text{ months}\):

\[N(t) = N_0 \cdot 2^{(t - t_0)/T_d} \qquad (T_d \approx 1.5\text{--}2\text{ years})\]

3. Major Implications for VLSI Technology:

  • Exponential Performance & Functionality: Higher transistor density and shorter channel lengths reduce gate delay (\(\tau \propto L\)) and enable entire multi-core processors, GPUs, and AI accelerators on a single System-on-Chip (SoC).
  • Exponentially Decreasing Cost per Function: Cost per logic gate dropped from dollars to nano-cents.
  • Power Density & Subthreshold Leakage Challenges: Breakdown of Dennard voltage scaling in sub-\(65\text{ nm}\) nodes led to high static leakage currents, dark silicon, and thermal dissipation limits.
2024 2024)1)c [5 Marks] Illustrate the sequential stages of the traditional VLSI design flow.
▼
Sol

The traditional VLSI design flow is a structured, sequential methodology divided into Front-End Design (Logical) and Back-End Design (Physical) stages:

  1. System Specification: Defines high-level chip requirements including functionality, target clock speed, power budget, die area, and I/O interfaces.
  2. Architectural Design: Partitions the system into major functional blocks (ALU, datapath, memory arrays, control units, bus interfaces) and selects algorithms.
  3. RTL Design & Behavioral Modeling: Expresses data flow and state transitions between registers using Hardware Description Languages (Verilog / VHDL).
  4. Functional Verification (Simulation): Tests the RTL code using testbenches to verify logical correctness before synthesis.
  5. Logic Synthesis: Translates the verified RTL code into a gate-level netlist mapped to a standard-cell technology library while meeting timing and area constraints.
  6. Gate-Level & Static Timing Verification: Verifies setup and hold timing constraints across all paths using Static Timing Analysis (STA).
  7. Floorplanning & Placement: Arranges macro blocks, I/O pads, power grids, and standard cells on the silicon die to minimize wire length and congestion.
  8. Clock Tree Synthesis (CTS) & Routing: Builds a balanced clock distribution network to minimize clock skew, followed by global and detailed metal routing.
  9. Parasitic Extraction & Physical Verification (DRC / LVS): Extracts parasitic \(R\) and \(C\) values for post-layout timing sign-off, checks Design Rule Checking (DRC) for \(\lambda\)-rule compliance, and runs Layout Versus Schematic (LVS).
  10. Tape-Out (GDSII): Generates the final photomask database (GDSII/OASIS format) for wafer fabrication.
2021-22 2021-22)1)a [4 Marks] Discuss the trends and the benefits of device size reduction in VLSI circuit design.
▼
Sol

1. Trends in Device Size Reduction:

  • Continuous scaling of minimum gate length (\(L\)) down to sub-\(3\text{ nm}\) regimes.
  • Transition from planar bulk MOSFETs to 3D architectural shapes like FinFETs and Gate-All-Around (GAA) Nanosheets to maintain electrostatic control.
  • Use of Extreme Ultraviolet (EUV) photolithography to achieve ultra-fine pitch sizing.

2. Benefits of Scaling:

  • Higher Integration Density: Millions/billions of more transistors can be integrated onto a single silicon die, allowing highly sophisticated system-on-chip architectures.
  • Faster Speed: Shorter channels decrease transistor carrier transit time, reducing gate propagation delays by approximately \(1/\beta\) (where \(\beta\) is the scaling factor).
  • Lower Dynamic Power: Parasitic capacitance shrinks, lowering the dynamic energy consumed per transition (\(E \propto V_{DD}^2 \cdot C_{\text{gate}}\)).
  • Lower Cost: Producing more functional units per wafer reduces the unit fabrication cost.

Module 2: CMOS & BiCMOS Gates

Inverters, NAND/NOR implementation, compound gates, switch levels, pass transistors, and BiCMOS/Domino logic structures.

💡 মনে রাখবে | CMOS & BiCMOS Gates Tips & Tricks
  • PUN & PDN Duality: Pull-up Network (PUN) সবসময় PMOS দিয়ে গঠিত হয় এবং এটি \(V_{DD}\)-এর সাথে যুক্ত থাকে। Pull-down Network (PDN) সবসময় NMOS দিয়ে গঠিত হয় এবং এটি Ground-এর সাথে যুক্ত থাকে।
  • NAND vs NOR Layout Rule: NAND গেটে NMOS-গুলো সিরিজে থাকে এবং PMOS-গুলো প্যারালালে থাকে। NOR গেটে NMOS-গুলো প্যারালালে থাকে এবং PMOS-গুলো সিরিজে থাকে। (Shortcut: NAND = NMOS Series).
  • Pass Transistor Logic: NMOS ভালো \(0\) (Strong 0) পাস করতে পারে কিন্তু দুর্বল \(1\) (Weak 1) পাস করে (\(V_{DD} - V_{th}\))। PMOS ভালো \(1\) (Strong 1) পাস করতে পারে কিন্তু দুর্বল \(0\) (Weak 0) পাস করে। পূর্ণ ভোল্টেজ পাসের জন্য Transmission Gate (NMOS + PMOS সমান্তরাল) ব্যবহার করা হয়।
  • Domino CMOS Rule: ডমিনো লজকে প্রতিটি ডাইনামিক স্টেজের পর একটি স্ট্যাটিক ইনভার্টার বসানো আবশ্যিক, যাতে পরবর্তী স্টেজ অসময়ে ডিসচার্জ না হয়।
  • BiCMOS Advantage (BiCMOS-এর সুবিধা): CMOS ড্রাইভের তুলনায় বাইপোলার (BJT) ট্রানজিস্টর লোড ক্যাপাসিট্যান্স খুব দ্রুত চার্জ করতে পারে। কিন্তু BJT-তে স্ট্যাটিক পাওয়ার ডিসিপেশন ও এরিয়া বেশি হয়। তাই ইনপুটে CMOS এবং আউটপুট ড্রাইভিং বাফারে BJT ব্যবহার করে BiCMOS তৈরি করা হয় (High Speed + Low Static Power)।
  • Switch Level Logic (সুইচ লেভেল লজিক): একটি NMOS সুইচ অন হয় যখন gate = 1, and অফ হয় gate = 0 হলে। PMOS সুইচ অন হয় gate = 0 হলে এবং অফ হয় gate = 1 হলে।

# Latch up in cmos #

Latch up is defined as the generation of low impedance path in cmos chips between \(V_{DD}\) (supply) and ground.

So, high current will get driven by \(V_{DD}\) toward ground and that may damage cmos circuit.

Note:

Describe latch up using SCR

If we are apply \(V_{DD}\) to anode and ground (GND) in cathode. So SCR is forward bias. When we apply a pulse on gate then SCR is on. If we removed the SCR gate pulse, but the SCR conducting current \(V_{DD}\) to ground. This is called latch up in SCR.

# Latch up in cmos Circuit #

Fig: Latch-up effect in p-well structure — CMOS cross section with n+/p+ regions, parasitic transistors T1, T2, resistors RS, RP, p-well and n-substrate labeled, forming parasitic SCR path between \(V_{DD}\) and \(V_{SS}\)

Latch-up is a condition in which the parasitic components give rise to the establishment of low-resistance conducting path between \(V_{DD}\) and \(V_{SS}\) with disastrous results.

There are, in effect, two transistors and two resistance which form a path between \(V_{DD}\) and \(V_{SS}\). If sufficient substrate current flows to generate enough voltage across

Page 60
📺 Watch Lecture CMOS Latch-Up: Latch-Up in CMOS & Parasitic SCR Prevention (Engineering Funda) ↗

page 60

\(R_s\) to turn on transistor T1, this will then draw current through \(R_p\) and if the voltage developed is sufficient, T2 will also turn on, establishing a self-sustaining low-resistance path between the supply rails. If the current gains of the two transistors are such that \(\beta_1 \times \beta_2 > 1\), latch-up may occur.

  2. If \(V_{\text{in}}\) or \(V_{\text{out}}\) < GND — [When \(V_{\text{out}}\)<GND, T1 is on. Then T2 is on. So the current is flowing \(V_{DD}\) to GND]

# latch-up prevention steps

  1. By gold impurities in substrate we can minimize latch up
  2. By using epitaxial layer at substrate.
  3. place substrate and well as close as possible
  4. Avoid forward bias of source and drain.
  5. place pmos close to \(V_{DD}\) and nmos close to ground.
Page S-3 (Supp)
📺 Watch Lecture Domino & Dynamic Logic: Domino Logic & Dynamic CMOS Logic Working (Engineering Funda) ↗

Synthesis of Combinational Logic: Dynamic, Clocked CMOS, and Domino Logic

1. Dynamic CMOS Logic:

Dynamic CMOS logic uses temporary charge storage on node capacitances to evaluate logic functions instead of relying on static pull-up networks. A basic dynamic gate consists of a Pull-Down Network (PDN), a precharge PMOS transistor, and an evaluate NMOS transistor:

  • Precharge Phase (\(\phi = 0\)): The precharge PMOS is ON, and the evaluate NMOS is OFF. The output node charges to \(V_{DD}\) regardless of input states.
  • Evaluate Phase (\(\phi = 1\)): The precharge PMOS is OFF, and the evaluate NMOS is ON. If the PDN conducts, the output node discharges to ground. If the PDN is OFF, the output remains high, held by the node capacitance.

Advantages: Requires only \(N+2\) transistors (compared to \(2N\) in static CMOS), has smaller layout area, and lower input capacitance.
Disadvantages: Charge sharing between internal nodes, charge leakage, and the cascading problem.

2. Clocked CMOS (\(C^2MOS\)) Logic:

Clocked CMOS logic incorporates clocked transistors in series with the standard static CMOS pull-up and pull-down networks. When clock \(\phi = 1\), the gate behaves like standard static CMOS. When \(\phi = 0\), both pull-up and pull-down paths are cut off, holding the previous state at the output. This is widely used in sequential elements to prevent races and overlap hazards.

3. CMOS Domino Logic:

The cascading problem in Dynamic CMOS arises because when evaluate begins, a dynamic stage output transitions from \(1 \to 0\). If this output drives a second dynamic stage, the transient high voltage at the input can discharge the second stage's output before the first stage can complete its evaluate transition. Once a dynamic node is discharged, it cannot be recharged during that cycle.

The Domino Solution: We insert a standard static CMOS inverter at the output of every dynamic stage. During precharge (\(\phi=0\)), the dynamic output is \(1\), so the inverter output is \(0\). When evaluation starts (\(\phi=1\)), the inputs to all subsequent stages are \(0\). The outputs can only transition from \(0 \to 1\) sequentially, cascading like a row of falling dominoes. This prevents premature discharge and enables seamless cascading.

Fig: CMOS Domino Logic Structure (Two Cascaded Stages)

NMOS Inverters with Resistive and Transistor Loads

1. NMOS Inverter with Resistive Load:

Uses an active enhancement-mode NMOS pull-down and a passive resistor \(R_L\) as a pull-up.
Drawback: High static power dissipation when input is high (current flows continuously through resistor) and requires huge silicon area to implement large resistors.

2. NMOS Inverter with Saturated Enhancement Load:

Replaces the resistor with an enhancement NMOS load transistor with its gate tied to \(V_{DD}\).
Drawback: The output high level is degraded to \(V_{OH} = V_{DD} - V_{th}\) due to threshold voltage drop (body effect increases this degradation further).

3. NMOS Inverter with Depletion Load:

Uses a depletion-mode NMOS (gate tied to source) as a pull-up load. Since the gate is tied to source, \(V_{GS} = 0\), keeping the load transistor constantly ON. It acts as a current source, pulling the output fully to \(V_{DD}\) (no threshold degradation) and offering faster switching speeds.

4. Pseudo-NMOS Inverter:

Uses a single PMOS transistor (gate tied to GND) as a pull-up load. It behaves like depletion load NMOS but is fabricated in standard CMOS technology without requiring depletion implants, saving masking steps.

2024 2024)2)a [5 Marks] Draw some switch logic arrangements with the help of CMOS.
▼
Sol

In CMOS VLSI design, MOS transistors are used as voltage-controlled switches to implement compact combinational and routing networks:

  1. NMOS & PMOS Pass Switches:
    • NMOS Switch (Active-High Gate \(C = 1\)): Passes a strong logic 0 (\(0\text{ V}\)) but a degraded logic 1 (\(V_{\text{out}} = V_{DD} - V_{\text{thn}}\)) due to threshold voltage drop.
    • PMOS Switch (Active-Low Gate \(\overline{C} = 0\)): Passes a strong logic 1 (\(V_{DD}\)) but a degraded logic 0 (\(V_{\text{out}} = |V_{\text{thp}}|\)).
  2. CMOS Transmission Gate (TG): Connects an NMOS and a PMOS transistor in parallel driven by complementary control signals (\(C\) and \(\overline{C}\)). Both logic `0` and logic `1` are transmitted losslessly over the full rail-to-rail swing (\(0\text{ V}\) to \(V_{DD}\)).
  3. Common Switch Logic Arrangements:
    • Series Switch Arrangement (AND Path): Switches in series conduct only when all control gates are active (\(Y = A \cdot B\)).
    • Parallel Switch Arrangement (OR Path): Switches in parallel conduct when any control gate is active (\(Y = A + B\)).
    • 2-to-1 Multiplexer Arrangement: Two transmission gates driven by \(S\) and \(\overline{S}\) select between inputs \(I_0\) and \(I_1\): \(Y = \overline{S} \cdot I_0 + S \cdot I_1\).
2024 2024)2)b [5 Marks] Determine the Pull-up to Pull-down ratio \((Z_{pu}/Z_{pd})\) for an NMOS inverter directly driven by another NMOS inverter.
▼
Sol

1. Operating Conditions at the Switching Threshold (\(V_{\text{in}} = V_{\text{inv}}\)):

Consider a depletion-load NMOS inverter directly driven by another identical NMOS inverter. For symmetrical logic margins, the inverter switching threshold is set at \(V_{\text{inv}} = 0.5\,V_{DD}\), where the input \(V_{\text{in}} = 0.5\,V_{DD}\) produces an output \(V_{\text{out}} = 0.5\,V_{DD}\):

  • Pull-Down Enhancement Driver (in Saturation): Since \(V_{DS,pd} = V_{\text{out}} = 0.5\,V_{DD} > V_{GS,pd} - V_{td} = 0.5\,V_{DD} - 0.2\,V_{DD}\), the pull-down NMOS operates in saturation:
    \[I_{DS,pd} = \frac{K_n W_{pd}}{2 L_{pd}} (V_{\text{inv}} - V_{td})^2 = \frac{\beta_{pd}}{2} (0.5\,V_{DD} - V_{td})^2\]
  • Pull-Up Depletion Load (in Resistive / Unsaturated Region): With its gate tied to its source (\(V_{GS,pu} = 0\text{ V}\)) and \(V_{DS,pu} = V_{DD} - V_{\text{out}} = 0.5\,V_{DD} < |V_{tp}| = 0.6\,V_{DD}\), the depletion load operates in the linear (resistive) region:
    \[I_{DS,pu} = \beta_{pu} \left[ (-V_{tp}) V_{DS,pu} - \frac{V_{DS,pu}^2}{2} \right]\]
    (Alternatively, using the standard Puknell simplified saturation-equivalence model at \(V_{GS,pu} = 0\)):
    \[I_{DS,pu} = \frac{\beta_{pu}}{2} (-V_{tp})^2 \quad \text{and} \quad I_{DS,pd} = \frac{\beta_{pd}}{2} (V_{\text{inv}} - V_{td})^2\]

2. Equating Pull-Up and Pull-Down Currents (\(I_{DS,pu} = I_{DS,pd}\)):

Since channel length-to-width ratio is \(Z = L/W\) and \(\beta = \mu \varepsilon / (t_{\text{ox}} Z) \propto 1/Z\), equating the currents yields:

\[\frac{1}{Z_{pu}} (-V_{tp})^2 = \frac{1}{Z_{pd}} (V_{\text{inv}} - V_{td})^2 \implies \frac{Z_{pu}}{Z_{pd}} = \left(\frac{-V_{tp}}{V_{\text{inv}} - V_{td}}\right)^2\]

3. Numerical Evaluation with Standard NMOS Parameters:

Substituting standard normalized values \(V_{\text{inv}} = 0.5\,V_{DD}\), enhancement threshold \(V_{td} = 0.2\,V_{DD}\), and depletion threshold \(V_{tp} = -0.6\,V_{DD}\):

\[\frac{Z_{pu}}{Z_{pd}} = \left(\frac{0.6\,V_{DD}}{0.5\,V_{DD} - 0.2\,V_{DD}}\right)^2 = \left(\frac{0.6}{0.3}\right)^2 = (2)^2 = 4\]
\[\therefore \quad \frac{Z_{pu}}{Z_{pd}} = 4 : 1 \quad \text{(for an NMOS inverter directly driven by another NMOS inverter)}\]

Note: If the NMOS inverter is driven through one or more pass transistors (which drop the input high voltage by \(V_{tp}\) to \(V_{DD} - V_{tp}\)), the required ratio increases to \(Z_{pu}/Z_{pd} = 8 : 1\).

2024 2024)2)c [4 Marks] Justify why pseudo-NMOS is often considered a trade-off between area efficiency, power dissipation, and speed.
▼
Sol

A Pseudo-NMOS gate replaces the bulky \(N\)-transistor PMOS pull-up network of complementary CMOS with a single permanently-ON PMOS load transistor whose gate is grounded (\(V_{G,p} = 0\text{ V}\)), creating a fundamental engineering trade-off across three metrics:

  1. Area Efficiency & Lower Input Capacitance (Advantage):
    • An \(N\)-input Pseudo-NMOS gate requires only \(N + 1\) transistors (\(N\) NMOS pull-down drivers + \(1\) PMOS load) compared to \(2N\) transistors in static CMOS.
    • Each input drives only one NMOS gate instead of both an NMOS and a PMOS gate, cutting input gate capacitance by more than half and greatly simplifying interconnect routing in wide fan-in NOR/ROM/PLA structures.
  2. Static Power Dissipation & Reduced Noise Margin (Penalty):
    • Because the PMOS load is always ON (\(V_{SGp} = V_{DD}\)), whenever the pull-down network conducts (output logic `0`), a continuous DC current \(I_{\text{static}}\) flows directly from \(V_{DD}\) to \(\text{GND}\), causing significant static power dissipation: \(P_{\text{static}} = V_{DD} \cdot I_{DS,p}\).
    • Furthermore, the resistive divider between the PMOS load and NMOS driver prevents the output from reaching \(0\text{ V}\) (\(V_{OL} > 0\text{ V}\)), degrading the low noise margin \(NM_L = V_{IL} - V_{OL}\).
  3. Speed vs. Sizing Trade-Off:
    • Making the PMOS load wider (smaller \(Z_{pu}\)) increases pull-up charging current and reduces rise delay (\(\tau_r\)), improving speed.
    • However, a stronger PMOS raises \(V_{OL}\) (risking logic failure if \(V_{OL} > V_{tn}\)) and increases static power dissipation. Conversely, making the PMOS narrower lowers static power and improves \(V_{OL}\), but makes the low-to-high transition (\(\tau_r\)) very slow.
MetricComplementary CMOS (\(2N\))Pseudo-NMOS (\(N+1\))
Transistor Count & AreaHigh (\(2N\) transistors)Compact (\(N+1\) transistors)
Static Power (\(P_{\text{static}}\))Zero (\(0\text{ W}\) ideal)High when output is Low (`0`)
Output Logic Low (\(V_{OL}\))\(0\text{ V}\) (Ratioless)\(V_{OL} > 0\text{ V}\) (Ratioed: requires \(Z_{pu}/Z_{pd} \approx 3:1\))
2024 2024)5)b [4 Marks] Explain the working principle of CMOS inverter.
▼
Sol

1. Circuit Structure:

A complementary MOS (CMOS) inverter consists of an enhancement-mode PMOS pull-up transistor (source connected to \(V_{DD}\)) and an enhancement-mode NMOS pull-down transistor (source connected to \(\text{GND}\)). Their gates are connected together to form the input \(V_{\text{in}}\), and their drains are tied together to form the output \(V_{\text{out}}\).

2. Switching Operation:

  • Case 1 — Logic 0 Input (\(V_{\text{in}} = 0\text{ V}\)):
    • For NMOS: \(V_{GSn} = 0\text{ V} < V_{tn} \implies\) NMOS is OFF (Cutoff), isolating \(V_{\text{out}}\) from \(\text{GND}\).
    • For PMOS: \(V_{SGp} = V_{DD} > |V_{tp}| \implies\) PMOS is ON (Linear), pulling the output all the way up to \(V_{\text{out}} = V_{DD}\) (Strong Logic `1`).
  • Case 2 — Logic 1 Input (\(V_{\text{in}} = V_{DD}\)):
    • For PMOS: \(V_{SGp} = 0\text{ V} < |V_{tp}| \implies\) PMOS is OFF (Cutoff), isolating \(V_{\text{out}}\) from \(V_{DD}\).
    • For NMOS: \(V_{GSn} = V_{DD} > V_{tn} \implies\) NMOS is ON (Linear), discharging the output completely to \(V_{\text{out}} = 0\text{ V}\) (Strong Logic `0`).

3. Five Operating Regions of the Voltage Transfer Characteristic (VTC):

  • Region A (\(0 \le V_{\text{in}} < V_{tn}\)): NMOS is Cutoff, PMOS is Linear \(\implies V_{\text{out}} = V_{DD}\).
  • Region B (\(V_{tn} \le V_{\text{in}} < V_{\text{inv}}\)): NMOS enters Saturation, PMOS in Linear region; \(V_{\text{out}}\) begins to drop slowly.
  • Region C (\(V_{\text{in}} = V_{\text{inv}} \approx 0.5\,V_{DD}\)): Both NMOS and PMOS operate in Saturation; steep vertical transition with high gain.
  • Region D (\(V_{\text{inv}} < V_{\text{in}} \le V_{DD} - |V_{tp}|\)): NMOS enters Linear region, PMOS is in Saturation.
  • Region E (\(V_{\text{in}} > V_{DD} - |V_{tp}|\)): PMOS is Cutoff, NMOS is Linear \(\implies V_{\text{out}} = 0\text{ V}\).
2024 2024)5)c [4 Marks] What is pass transistor and transmission gate logic? Explain 'OR' gate logic using transmission gate and 'AND' gate logic using pass transistor.
▼
Sol

1. Pass Transistor Logic (PTL) vs. Transmission Gate (TG) Logic:

  • Pass Transistor Logic (PTL): Uses single NMOS (or PMOS) transistors as switches where signals can be applied to both the gate and the source/drain terminals. Reduces transistor count drastically, though an NMOS pass switch suffers a threshold drop (\(V_{\text{out,max}} = V_{DD} - V_{\text{thn}}\)) when passing a logic `1`.
  • Transmission Gate (TG) Logic: Connects an NMOS and a PMOS transistor in parallel with complementary gate signals (\(C\) and \(\overline{C}\)). Eliminates the threshold voltage drop and passes both logic `0` and logic `1` with full rail-to-rail voltage swing.

2. 2-Input AND Gate Using NMOS Pass Transistor Logic:

  • Consists of two NMOS switches controlled by \(B\) and \(\overline{B}\):
    • When \(B = 1\) (\(\overline{B} = 0\)): The upper NMOS switch is ON and the lower NMOS is OFF, passing input \(A\) directly to the output (\(Y = A\)).
    • When \(B = 0\) (\(\overline{B} = 1\)): The upper NMOS is OFF and the lower NMOS is ON, connecting the output \(Y\) to \(\text{GND}\) (\(Y = 0\)) so the output never floats in a high-impedance state.
  • Combined Boolean output: \(Y = A \cdot B + 0 \cdot \overline{B} = A \cdot B\).

3. 2-Input OR Gate Using CMOS Transmission Gate (TG) Logic:

  • Uses a CMOS Transmission Gate controlled by input \(A\) (and \(\overline{A}\)) plus a pull-up switch:
    • When \(A = 0\) (\(\overline{A} = 1\)): The Transmission Gate is ON, routing input \(B\) directly to the output (\(Y = B\)).
    • When \(A = 1\) (\(\overline{A} = 0\)): The Transmission Gate is OFF, and the auxiliary transistor connects the output directly to \(V_{DD}\) (logic `1`), yielding \(Y = 1\).
  • Combined Boolean output: \(Y = \overline{A} \cdot B + A \cdot 1 = A + B\).
2024 2024)6)c [4 Marks] Draw the circuit and stick diagram of 2-input NAND gate using BiCMOS inverter.
▼
Sol

1. BiCMOS 2-Input NAND Gate Architecture & Operation:

A 2-input BiCMOS NAND gate combines the zero-static-power, high-input-impedance advantages of CMOS logic gates with the high current-driving capability (\(g_m\)) of bipolar NPN transistors (\(Q_1, Q_2\)) to drive large capacitive loads rapidly:

  • Pull-Up Stage (When \(A = 0\) or \(B = 0\)): Parallel PMOS transistors (\(M_{pA}, M_{pB}\)) conduct and supply base current to the upper NPN BJT \(Q_1\), turning \(Q_1\) ON strongly to charge the output node \(Y = \overline{A \cdot B}\) toward \(V_{DD}\). Meanwhile, series NMOS transistors ensure the base of \(Q_2\) is discharged so \(Q_2\) remains OFF.
  • Pull-Down Stage (When \(A = 1\) and \(B = 1\)): Both PMOS transistors turn OFF. Series NMOS transistors (\(M_{nA1}, M_{nB1}\)) connect the collector/output node to the base of the lower NPN BJT \(Q_2\), turning \(Q_2\) ON to rapidly discharge the capacitive load to \(\text{GND}\) (\(Y = 0\)), while auxiliary NMOS switches (\(M_{nA2}, M_{nB2}\)) discharge the base of \(Q_1\).
2021-22 2021-22)2)a [4 Marks] Explain why static power dissipation in a CMOS inverter is zero.
▼
Sol

Static power dissipation (\(P_{\text{static}}\)) in a digital circuit occurs when there is a continuous conduction path from the supply rail (\(V_{DD}\)) to the ground (GND) under stable state conditions.

For a complementary MOS (CMOS) inverter consisting of one PMOS pull-up and one NMOS pull-down transistor, the operation is split into two static states:

  • Input Low (\(V_{\text{in}} = 0\text{ V}\)): The PMOS is turned fully ON, while the NMOS gate-to-source voltage is 0V, keeping the NMOS fully OFF. The PMOS pulls the output to \(V_{DD}\). Since the NMOS acts as an open switch, no current flows from \(V_{DD}\) to GND.
  • Input High (\(V_{\text{in}} = V_{DD}\)): The NMOS is turned fully ON, while the PMOS source-to-gate voltage is 0V, keeping the PMOS fully OFF. The NMOS discharges the output to GND. Since the PMOS acts as an open switch, no current flows from \(V_{DD}\) to GND.

In either stable logic state, one of the two series-connected networks is completely non-conductive. The static leakage current (\(I_{\text{static}}\)) is zero in the ideal switch view, resulting in zero static power dissipation:

\[P_{\text{static}} = I_{\text{static}} \times V_{DD} = 0 \times V_{DD} = 0\text{ W}\]
2021-22 2021-22)2)b [5 Marks] Elucidate the operation of a 2-input CMOS nand gate assuming the switch view of transistors.
▼
Sol

A 2-input CMOS NAND gate is designed using a parallel Pull-Up Network (PUN) of two PMOS transistors (\(M_{pA}, M_{pB}\)) and a series Pull-Down Network (PDN) of two NMOS transistors (\(M_{nA}, M_{nB}\)).

Using the switch model (where 0 turns PMOS ON and NMOS OFF, and 1 turns PMOS OFF and NMOS ON), the operation for all input combinations is:

  1. A = 0, B = 0: Both PMOS switches \(M_{pA}\) and \(M_{pB}\) are CLOSED (ON). Both NMOS switches \(M_{nA}\) and \(M_{nB}\) are OPEN (OFF). The output node is connected to \(V_{DD}\) through both PMOS paths. Output Y = 1.
  2. A = 0, B = 1: PMOS switch \(M_{pA}\) is CLOSED (ON) and \(M_{pB}\) is OPEN. NMOS \(M_{nA}\) is OPEN and \(M_{nB}\) is CLOSED. The output node remains connected to \(V_{DD}\) through \(M_{pA}\). Output Y = 1.
  3. A = 1, B = 0: PMOS \(M_{pA}\) is OPEN and \(M_{pB}\) is CLOSED (ON). NMOS \(M_{nA}\) is CLOSED and \(M_{nB}\) is OPEN. The output node remains connected to \(V_{DD}\) through \(M_{pB}\). Output Y = 1.
  4. A = 1, B = 1: Both PMOS switches are OPEN. Both NMOS switches \(M_{nA}\) and \(M_{nB}\) are CLOSED (ON), establishing a low-impedance path to GND. Output Y = 0.

This matches the NAND logic operation truth table.

2021-22 2021-22)3)a [5 Marks] Explain the Latch-up phenomena in CMOS circuits and how to prevent it.
▼
Sol

1. Latch-up Phenomena:

Latch-up is the creation of a low-impedance parasitic path between \(V_{DD}\) and GND in bulk CMOS circuits, causing high currents that can destroy the chip. It is caused by the activation of parasitic bipolar junction transistors (BJTs) that form a Silicon Controlled Rectifier (SCR) structure:

  • A parasitic lateral PNP transistor (emitter = PMOS p+ source, base = n-well, collector = p-substrate).
  • A parasitic vertical NPN transistor (emitter = NMOS n+ source, base = p-substrate, collector = n-well).

These two BJTs form a feedback loop. If noise, overvoltage spikes, or leakage current causes current to flow through the substrate or well resistance (\(R_{\text{sub}}\) or \(R_{\text{well}}\)), it will turn on one BJT. This BJT then supplies base current to the second BJT, turning it on. This cycle keeps both BJTs in saturation, short-circuiting \(V_{DD}\) and GND.

2. Prevention Methods:

  • Substrate/Well Contacts (Taps): Placing frequent, highly-doped well contacts to \(V_{DD}\) and substrate contacts to GND close to the sources of transistors. This reduces \(R_{\text{sub}}\) and \(R_{\text{well}}\), preventing the voltage drop from reaching the 0.7V required to turn on the BJTs.
  • Guard Rings: Placing p+ guard rings connected to GND around NMOS, and n+ guard rings connected to \(V_{DD}\) around PMOS to collect minority carriers before they can reach BJT bases.
  • Isolation Technologies: Using trench isolation (STI) or Silicon-on-Insulator (SOI) substrates to physically isolate NMOS and PMOS devices.
2021-22 2021-22)4)c [5 Marks] Describe the advantage the Pseudo NMOS inverter present compare to NMOS inverter.
▼
Sol

A depletion-load NMOS inverter uses a depletion-mode NMOS load (gate connected to source) which requires a negative threshold voltage. A Pseudo-NMOS inverter uses an enhancement-mode PMOS load (gate connected to GND).

Advantages of Pseudo-NMOS:

  • Easier Fabrication: Depletion-load NMOS requires an extra ion-implantation step and mask to selectively shift the threshold voltage of load transistors. Pseudo-NMOS uses standard enhancement PMOS, eliminating this step, which reduces fabrication cost and complexity.
  • Saves Layout Area: Pseudo-NMOS offers similar area efficiency (requires only \(N + 1\) transistors compared to \(2N\) in static CMOS) while remaining compatible with standard CMOS processes.
  • Better noise margins: Pseudo-NMOS typically exhibits a sharper Voltage Transfer Characteristic (VTC) transition compared to saturated enhancement-load NMOS.

Module 3: VLSI Fabrication & Reliability

Photolithography steps, etching, ion implantation, NMOS, CMOS, and BiCMOS process flows, Latch-up & Electromigration details, and fabrication interfaces.

💡 মনে রাখবে | Fabrication & Reliability Tips & Tricks
  • Fabrication Flow Pattern: Oxidation (সিলিকন ডাইঅক্সাইড তৈরি) \(\to\) Photolithography (প্যাটার্ন তৈরি) \(\to\) Etching (অপ্রয়োজনীয় অক্সাইড দূরীকরণ) \(\to\) Diffusion / Ion Implantation (ডোপিং) \(\to\) Metallization (কানেকশন)।
  • Latch-up Root Cause: CMOS সার্কিটে পরজীবী (parasitic) p-n-p-n ডায়োড বা SCR তৈরি হয়ে \(V_{DD}\) এবং Ground শর্ট হয়ে যাওয়াকে ল্যাচ-আপ বলে।
  • Latch-up Prevention: সাবস্ট্রেটে বেশি বেশি করে Guard Rings ব্যবহার করা এবং \(V_{DD}\) ও Ground-এর মধ্যে দূরত্ব বাড়ানো।
  • Electromigration Rule: হাই কারেন্ট ডেনসিটির কারণে তারের ধাতু ক্ষয় হওয়া প্রতিরোধে Black's Equation (\(MTF \propto 1/J^2\)) ব্যবহার করে সর্বোচ্চ কারেন্ট ডেনসিটি \(J \le 1\text{ mA/}\mu\text{m}^2\)-এ সীমাবদ্ধ রাখা উচিত।
  • Etching Types (ইচিং-এর শর্টকাট):
    • Wet Etching: কেমিক্যাল লিকুইড ব্যবহার করা হয়। এটি আইসোট্রপিক (সবদিকে সমানভাবে কাটে), যা সাব-মাইক্রন লেভেলের জন্য ভালো নয়।
    • Dry Etching (RIE): প্লাজমা গ্যাস ব্যবহার করা হয়। এটি অ্যানাইসোট্রপিক (শুধুমাত্র লম্বভাবে কাটে), যা নিখুঁত সাব-মাইক্রন প্যাটার্ন তৈরির জন্য উপযুক্ত।
  • Oxidation Types (অক্সিডেশন শর্টকাট): Wet Oxidation (জলীয় বাষ্প ব্যবহার করে দ্রুত কিন্তু কম ঘনত্বের অক্সাইড তৈরি) বনাম Dry Oxidation (বিশুদ্ধ অক্সিজেন গ্যাস ব্যবহার করে ধীরগতির কিন্তু অতি উচ্চমানের পাতলা গেট অক্সাইড তৈরি)।

ch-6 # MOS Fabrication / Process Technology #

Fabrication is the process of building/manufacturing integrated circuits on a semiconductor wafer (usually silicon).

# Main steps of MOS fabrication —

  1. Wafer preparation.
  2. Epitaxy.
  3. Oxidation.
  4. Photolithography.
  5. Diffusion / \(I_{\text{on}}\) implantation.
  6. Metallization.
  7. Passivation / Encapsulation.

# 1. Wafer preparation —

* Silicon is extracted from silica (SiO2) found in sand, and purified to electronic grade silicon (EGS, purity 99.9999999%).

* A single crystal ingot is grown using the Czochralski (CZ) process.

* The ingot is sliced into thin circular wafers, then polished.

Fig: Czochralski crystal growth — seed crystal dipped into molten silicon, pulled & rotated to form cylindrical ingot

│ • seed crystal dipped into molten silicon, pulled &      │
│   rotated to form cylindrical ingot                      │

page 104

# 2. Epitaxy —

* Epitaxy is the process of growing a thin single-crystal layer of silicon on top of the wafer, with controlled doping, that has the same crystal orientation as the substrate.

* This epitaxial layer forms the active region where the actual devices (transistors) are built.

# 3. Oxidation —

* The wafer surface is exposed to oxygen or steam at high temperature (900–1200°C) to grow a layer of SiO2 (silicon dioxide).

* This SiO2 layer acts as an insulator and also as a mask during selective doping steps.

* Two types:

  1. Dry oxidation (using O2) — slow, produces high quality/dense oxide, used for thin gate oxides.
  2. Wet oxidation (using H2O/steam) — fast, produces thicker oxide, used for field/isolation oxide.
Fig: Oxidation furnace — wafers in quartz boat inside tube furnace, O2/H2O gas flow, heating coils

│ TECHNICAL SCHEMATIC SPECIFICATION                        │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Block : Oxidation furnace                                │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ • wafers in quartz boat inside tube furnace, O2/H2O gas  │
│   flow, heating coils                                    │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
