Structured Design: Parity Generator & Bus Arbitration Logic

1. Parity Generator structured design:

A parity generator is a structured block of combinational logic used to compute an even or odd parity bit for a data word. Parity generation is based on cascaded exclusive-OR (XOR) gates. For a 4-bit input word \((A, B, C, D)\), the parity bit \(P\) is generated by:

\[P = A \oplus B \oplus C \oplus D = (A \oplus B) \oplus (C \oplus D)\]

In structured VLSI layout, this XOR tree is implemented as a regular 2D array of cells. The standard XOR logic gate is structured to tile seamlessly with adjacent cells to minimize routing capacitance and propagation delay.

2. Bus Arbitration Logic:

Bus arbitration regulates access to a shared bus when multiple master devices request control. There are two common structured designs:

  1. Daisy Chain Arbiter: Devices are physically chained. The "bus grant" signal propagates through each device in order of priority. If a device has made a request, it blocks the signal; otherwise, it passes it to the next. The priority is structured purely by the physical cascade order.
  2. Priority Encoder Arbiter: Requests are routed to a central priority encoder block. The encoder outputs the binary address of the highest-priority active request. Structured layouts use PLA (Programmable Logic Array) matrices to decode priorities efficiently.
Fig: 4-bit Even Parity Generator XOR Tree
2024 2024)7)a [5 Marks] Design the bounding box for a single 4-bit register and the floor plan for a \(4 \times 4\) bit register array.
▼
Sol

1. Bounding Box Design for a 1-Bit Cell & Single 4-Bit Register:

  • In structured VLSI subsystem design, a Bounding Box represents the physical outline and port locations of a leaf cell so that cells can be tiled directly by abutment without extra inter-cell routing channels.
  • Each 1-bit pseudo-static register cell has horizontal rails for \(V_{DD}\), \(\text{GND}\), and the bidirectional Data Bus (\(\text{Bus}_i\)) in Metal, and vertical control lines for Read (\(\text{RD}\cdot\phi_1\)), Write (\(\text{WR}\cdot\phi_1\)), and Refresh (\(\phi_2\)) in Polysilicon.
  • Stacking four 1-bit cells vertically (Bit 0, Bit 1, Bit 2, Bit 3) forms the Bounding Box of a Single 4-Bit Register: the vertical \(\text{RD}\), \(\text{WR}\), and \(\phi_2\) control lines run continuously from top to bottom through all 4 bits, while the 4 horizontal data bus lines (\(\text{Bus}_0 \dots \text{Bus}_3\)) cross each corresponding bit slice.

2. Floorplan for a \(4 \times 4\)-Bit Register Array:

  • Four 4-bit registers (\(\text{Reg}_0, \text{Reg}_1, \text{Reg}_2, \text{Reg}_3\)) are placed side-by-side horizontally to form a regular \(4 \times 4\) array (4 words \(\times\) 4 bits per word = 16 bit cells).
  • Horizontal Bus Continuity: The four data bus lines (\(\text{Bus}_0, \text{Bus}_1, \text{Bus}_2, \text{Bus}_3\)) along with \(V_{DD}\) and \(\text{GND}\) run horizontally across all four register columns.
  • Vertical Control Selection: Each column \(j \in \{0,1,2,3\}\) has its own dedicated vertical Read Select (\(\text{RD}_j\)) and Write Select (\(\text{WR}_j\)) lines driven by the register decoder.
2021-22 2021-22)5)b [5 Marks] Design and limitation of various Bus system for 4 bit arithmetic processor.
▼
Sol

In a 4-bit VLSI arithmetic processor (datapath), registers, shifters, and the ALU are interconnected via shared horizontal metal tracks called Data Buses. Depending on the speed-area trade-off, three bus architectures are used:

  1. 1-Bus (Single-Bus) Datapath Architecture:
    • Design: All registers (\(R_0 \dots R_3\)), the ALU, and the shifter share a single 4-bit bidirectional bus. Because the ALU requires two simultaneous operands, temporary latches must hold Operand 1 and the ALU Output.
    • Execution Sequence (\(R_1 \leftarrow R_1 + R_2\)): Cycle 1: Read \(R_1\) onto Bus \(\to\) Latch into ALU Input. Cycle 2: Read \(R_2\) onto Bus \(\to\) Compute in ALU \(\to\) Latch result. Cycle 3: Drive ALU Latch onto Bus \(\to\) Write into \(R_1\).
    • Limitation: Very slow (requires 3 clock cycles per arithmetic instruction) due to bus contention, though it occupies the smallest silicon area.
  2. 2-Bus Datapath Architecture:
    • Design: Uses two 4-bit buses (e.g., Bus A and Bus B). Operands are read over the buses and latched into the ALU, or Bus A feeds the ALU while Bus B returns the result to the register file via a temporary buffer.
    • Limitation: Requires 2 clock cycles per instruction and still needs an intermediate latch to prevent race conditions between reading source registers and writing back the destination register.
  3. 3-Bus Datapath Architecture:
    • Design: Uses two dedicated source buses (Bus A for Operand 1, Bus B for Operand 2) feeding the two inputs of the ALU/Shifter simultaneously, and a third dedicated destination bus (Bus C) routing the ALU result directly back to the selected destination register.
    • Advantage & Limitation: Completes \(R_3 \leftarrow R_1 + R_2\) in a single clock cycle (\(1\text{ cycle}\)) without temporary ALU latches! However, routing \(3 \times 4 = 12\) horizontal metal bus lines across every bit-slice significantly increases silicon area, vertical cell pitch, and bus capacitance.

Design of 4-bit Binary Adders, truth tables, multiplexer-based adder logic, and carry chain optimizations.

💡 মনে রাখবে | Adder & ALU Sizing Shortcuts
  • Full Adder equations: Sum \(S_K = A_K \oplus B_K \oplus C_{K-1}\), Carry \(C_K = A_K B_K + C_{K-1}(A_K \oplus B_K)\)।
  • Carry Propagation Delay: ফুল অ্যাডারে সবচেয়ে বেশি সময় ক্যারি প্রোপাগেশনে লাগে। স্পিড বাড়ানোর জন্য ম্যানচেস্টার ক্যারি চেইন বা ক্যারি লুক-অ্যাহেড অ্যাডারের সাহায্য নেওয়া হয়।
  • Switch-Based Adder: পাস ট্রানজিস্টর বা ট্রান্সমিশন গেট ভিত্তিক অ্যাডারে সিরিজে ৪টির বেশি সুইচ বাফার ছাড়া রাখা উচিত নয়, কারণ ডিলে \(O(N^2)\) হারে বৃদ্ধি পায়।
  • Manchester Carry Chain (ম্যানচেস্টার ক্যারি চেইন): ক্যারি জেনারেশন (\(G = A \cdot B\)) এবং প্রোপাগেশন (\(P = A \oplus B\)) সিগন্যাল ব্যবহার করে সুইচের মাধ্যমে অতি দ্রুত ক্যারি পাস করার একটি ডাইনামিক সার্কিট। এতে ট্রান্সমিশন গেট ব্যবহার করায় ক্যারি ডিলে রৈখিকভাবে বাড়ে, যা স্ট্যান্ডার্ড ক্যারি রিপল অ্যাডারের চেয়ে অনেক দ্রুত।
  • ALU Design Shortcut: ALU-তে গাণিতিক ও লজিক অপারেশনগুলোর কন্ট্রোল লাইন বা সিলেক্টরগুলোকে এমনভাবে ডিজাইন করতে হয় যাতে একটি কমন সার্কিট (যেমন ফুল অ্যাডার) বিভিন্ন মোডে কাজ করতে পারে।

Adder element requirements

The adder requirements may be state thus:

\[\text{If } A_K = B_K \text{ then } S_K = C_{K-1}\]
\[\text{else, } S_K = \overline{C}_{K-1}\]

for the carry CK

\[\text{If } A_K = B_K \text{ then } C_K = A_K = B_K\]
\[\text{else } C_K = C_{K-1}\]

This relationship could also have been stated as:

Design of a General Purpose ALU Subsystem

1. ALU Architecture Slice:

A general-purpose Arithmetic Logic Unit (ALU) is designed by cascading 1-bit ALU slices. Each slice consists of an Arithmetic Unit (performing addition, subtraction, increment) and a Logic Unit (performing AND, OR, XOR, NOT), with multiplexers selecting the operation based on control inputs \(S_0, S_1, S_2\).

2. Multiplexer-Based ALU Slice Scheme:

A 1-bit ALU can be designed using a Full Adder and a 4-to-1 Multiplexer. Control lines configure the inputs to the Full Adder to perform logic functions:

  • To perform AND: Disable carry propagation, route \(A \cdot B\) to the output.
  • To perform ADD: Enable carry propagation, inputs are \(A\) and \(B\), yield Sum and Carry.
  • To perform XOR: Configure gates to pass \(A \oplus B\) directly.

2024 2024)7)b [5 Marks] Describe the Implementing of 4 bit ALU functions with an adder.
▼
Sol

1. Mathematical Principle of Implementing ALU Functions with a Standard Adder:

In VLSI subsystem design (Puknell & Eshraghian), a 4-bit Arithmetic Logic Unit (ALU) is implemented directly around a 4-bit parallel binary adder by controlling the inputs (\(A_k, B_k\)) and the inter-stage carry signals (\(C_{k-1}\)). For column \(k\), the standard full-adder equations are:

\[\text{Half-Adder: } H_k = A_k \oplus B_k = \overline{A}_k B_k + A_k \overline{B}_k\]
\[\text{Sum: } S_k = H_k \cdot \overline{C}_{k-1} + \overline{H}_k \cdot C_{k-1} \qquad\text{and}\qquad \text{New Carry: } C_k = A_k B_k + H_k C_{k-1}\]

2. Deriving Arithmetic & Logical Operations from \(S_k\) and \(C_k\):

  • Arithmetic Addition (\(A + B\)): Enable normal carry propagation (\(C_{k-1}\) ripples from stage \(k-1\), with \(C_{\text{in}} = 0\)). The adder computes \(S_k = A_k \oplus B_k \oplus C_{k-1}\) and carry-out \(C_k\).
  • Arithmetic Subtraction (\(A - B = A + \overline{B} + 1\)): Invert operand \(B\) (\(B_k \to \overline{B}_k\)) using input multiplexers, set initial carry-in \(C_{\text{in}} = 1\) (2's complement), and enable carry propagation.
  • Logical XOR (\(A \oplus B\)): Disable carry propagation and clamp the previous carry input to \(C_{k-1} = 0\) at every bit slice. Substituting \(C_{k-1} = 0\) into the Sum equation gives:
    \[S_k = H_k \cdot \overline{0} + \overline{H}_k \cdot 0 = H_k = A_k \oplus B_k\]
  • Logical XNOR (\(\overline{A \oplus B}\)): Clamp the previous carry input to \(C_{k-1} = 1\) at every bit slice. Substituting \(C_{k-1} = 1\) into the Sum equation gives:
    \[S_k = H_k \cdot \overline{1} + \overline{H}_k \cdot 1 = \overline{H}_k = \overline{A_k \oplus B_k}\]
  • Logical AND (\(A \cdot B\)): Clamp \(C_{k-1} = 0\) at every bit slice and take the output from the New Carry (\(C_k\)) terminal:
    \[C_k = A_k B_k + H_k \cdot 0 = A_k \cdot B_k\]
  • Logical OR (\(A + B\)): Clamp \(C_{k-1} = 1\) at every bit slice and take the output from the New Carry (\(C_k\)) terminal:
    \[C_k = A_k B_k + H_k \cdot 1 = A_k B_k + \overline{A}_k B_k + A_k \overline{B}_k = A_k + B_k\]
ALU OperationOperand \(B\) InputPrevious Carry \(C_{k-1}\)Selected Output TerminalResulting Function
ADD\(B_k\)Ripple (\(C_{\text{in}} = 0\))Sum (\(S_k\))\(A + B\)
SUB\(\overline{B}_k\)Ripple (\(C_{\text{in}} = 1\))Sum (\(S_k\))\(A - B\) (2's comp.)
XOR\(B_k\)Clamped to \(0\)Sum (\(S_k\))\(A_k \oplus B_k\)
XNOR\(B_k\)Clamped to \(1\)Sum (\(S_k\))\(\overline{A_k \oplus B_k}\)
AND\(B_k\)Clamped to \(0\)Carry (\(C_k\))\(A_k \cdot B_k\)
OR\(B_k\)Clamped to \(1\)Carry (\(C_k\))\(A_k + B_k\)
2021-22 2021-22)5)c [4 Marks] Draw the two types of switch that represent for 4 bit shifter for data transfer.
▼
Sol

A 4-bit Crossbar or Barrel Shifter routes any of the 4 input bus lines (\(I_0, I_1, I_2, I_3\)) to any of the 4 output bus lines (\(O_0, O_1, O_2, O_3\)) using a \(4 \times 4\) matrix of MOS crosspoint switches. The two types of switches used at each crosspoint are:

  1. NMOS Pass-Transistor Crosspoint Switch:
    • Structure: Uses a single enhancement-mode NMOS transistor at each crosspoint \((i, j)\), with its gate connected to the shift control line \(S_k\), source connected to input bus \(I_i\), and drain connected to output bus \(O_j\).
    • Advantage: Very compact layout area (only 1 transistor and 1 control wire per crosspoint).
    • Limitation: Suffers from threshold voltage degradation when passing logic `1` (\(V_{\text{out,max}} = V_{DD} - V_{\text{thn}}\)) and capacitive coupling from control lines.
  2. CMOS Transmission Gate (TG) Crosspoint Switch:
    • Structure: Uses an NMOS transistor and a PMOS transistor connected in parallel between \(I_i\) and \(O_j\), driven by complementary shift control signals \(S_k\) and \(\overline{S}_k\).
    • Advantage: Provides lossless rail-to-rail signal transfer (\(0\text{ V}\) to \(V_{DD}\)) with lower ON-resistance and faster charging of the output bus capacitance.
    • Limitation: Requires 2 transistors, \(n\)-well separation, and complementary control routing (\(S_k, \overline{S}_k\)).

1. Truth Table for the \(k\)-th Column of a Binary Full Adder:

000000
010110
100110
110001
001010
011101
101101
111011

2. Boolean Equations & Multiplexer-Based CMOS Adder Logic:

From the truth table, defining the half-adder signal \(H_k = \overline{A}_k B_k + A_k \overline{B}_k = A_k \oplus B_k\), the Sum and New Carry equations are:

\[S_k = H_k \cdot \overline{C}_{k-1} + \overline{H}_k \cdot C_{k-1} \qquad\text{and}\qquad C_k = A_k B_k + H_k C_{k-1}\]

Observing the table yields the Switch/Multiplexer Adder Requirements:

  • For Sum (\(S_k\)): If \(A_k = B_k\) (\(H_k = 0\)), then \(S_k = C_{k-1}\); else if \(A_k \neq B_k\) (\(H_k = 1\)), then \(S_k = \overline{C}_{k-1}\).
  • For Carry (\(C_k\)): If \(A_k = B_k\) (\(H_k = 0\)), then \(C_k = A_k = B_k\) (carry generate/kill); else if \(A_k \neq B_k\) (\(H_k = 1\)), then \(C_k = C_{k-1}\) (carry propagate).

3. 4-Bit CMOS Adder Subsystem Design:

Four 1-bit CMOS Full Adder elements (\(\text{FA}_0, \text{FA}_1, \text{FA}_2, \text{FA}_3\)) are cascaded in series along the 4-bit processor data path so that \(C_{\text{out}}\) (\(C_k\)) of bit \(k-1\) feeds \(C_{\text{in}}\) (\(C_{k-1}\)) of bit \(k\), producing the 4-bit sum \((S_3 S_2 S_1 S_0)\) and final carry-out \(C_3\).

2021-22 2021-22)6)b [5 Marks] Design the 4 bit Braun Multiplier.
▼
Sol

1. Mathematical Principle of the \(4 \times 4\)-Bit Braun Array Multiplier:

A Braun Multiplier is a high-speed, Carry-Save parallel array multiplier designed for unsigned binary numbers. Given two 4-bit unsigned operands \(A = a_3 a_2 a_1 a_0\) (multiplicand) and \(B = b_3 b_2 b_1 b_0\) (multiplier), the 8-bit product \(P = P_7 P_6 P_5 P_4 P_3 P_2 P_1 P_0\) is expressed as:

\[P = \sum_{j=0}^{3} \sum_{i=0}^{3} (a_i \cdot b_j)\, 2^{i+j}\]

2. Hardware Architecture & Component Count:

  • Partial Product Generator (\(16\) AND Gates): An array of \(4 \times 4 = 16\) two-input AND gates computes all bit-products \(p_{ij} = a_i \cdot b_j\) in parallel in a single gate delay.
  • Carry-Save Adder (CSA) Array (\(3\) Rows of Adders): Instead of propagating carries horizontally across each row, each Full Adder passes its Sum output vertically/diagonally and its Carry output directly down to the next row of the same bit weight:
    • Row 1: Sums \(a_i b_0\) and \(a_i b_1\) using \(4\) Full Adders (with \(C_{\text{in}} = 0\), acting as Half Adders); outputs \(P_1\) (\(P_0 = a_0 b_0\) directly).
    • Row 2: Sums Row 1 outputs with \(a_i b_2\) using \(4\) Full Adders; outputs \(P_2\).
    • Row 3: Sums Row 2 outputs with \(a_i b_3\) using \(4\) Full Adders; outputs \(P_3\).
  • Final Carry-Propagate Adder (CPA — Row 4): A final 4-bit Ripple-Carry stage resolves the remaining sum and carry vectors to generate the upper product bits \(P_4, P_5, P_6, P_7\). Total adders required: \(12\) Full Adders + \(4\) Half Adders (or \(12\) Full Adders total when integrated).

3. Critical Path Delay:

\[T_{\text{mult}} = t_{\text{AND}} + (N - 1)\,t_{\text{CSA}} + (N - 1)\,t_{\text{carry}}\]

💡 মনে রাখবে | Memory & Sequential Design Tips
  • RAM Cell Comparison:
    • 3T DRAM: ১টি স্টোরেজ ক্যাপাসিটর এবং ৩টি ট্রানজিস্টর। রিড করার সময় ডেটা নষ্ট হয় না (Non-destructive read)।
    • 1T DRAM: ১টি ট্রানজিস্টর এবং ১টি ক্যাপাসিটর। রিড করার সময় ডেটা নষ্ট হয়ে যায় (Destructive read), তাই রিডের পর রিফ্রেশ করতে হয়।
    • 6T SRAM: ৪টি ইনভার্টার ল্যাচ এবং ২টি অ্যাক্সেস ট্রানজিস্টর। রিফ্রেশ করার প্রয়োজন হয় না, অনেক দ্রুত কাজ করে কিন্তু এরিয়া বেশি লাগে।
  • Mealy vs Moore FSM:
    • Moore Output: শুধুমাত্র বর্তমান স্টেটের ওপর নির্ভর করে। স্টেট পরিবর্তন না হওয়া পর্যন্ত আউটপুট স্থির থাকে (গ্লিচ-ফ্রি)।
    • Mealy Output: বর্তমান স্টেট এবং ইনপুট উভয়ের ওপর নির্ভর করে। ইনপুট পরিবর্তনের সাথে সাথে আউটপুটও পরিবর্তিত হতে পারে, তাই রিডিং-এ গ্লিচ বা নয়েজ আসতে পারে।
  • Static Noise Margin (SNM): SRAM মেমোরি সেলের রিড ও রাইট অপারেশনগুলোর সময় স্ট্যাবিলিটি পরিমাপ করার একটি মানদণ্ড। বাটারফ্লাই কার্ভ (Butterfly Curve) এঁকে এর মান বের করা হয়।
  • 2-Phase Clocking Rule: ফেইজ ১ (\(\phi_1\)) এবং ফেইজ ২ (\(\phi_2\)) এমনভাবে তৈরি করতে হবে যেন তারা কখনোই ওভারল্যাপ না করে (Non-overlapping)। ওভারল্যাপ করলে ডেটা রেস কন্ডিশন তৈরি হবে এবং ভুল ডেটা রাইট হবে।

System Timing Considerations

  1. A two-phase non-overlapping clock signal is assumed to be available, and this clock alone will be used throughout the system.
  2. Clock phase are to be identified as \(\phi_1\) and \(\phi_2\) where \(\phi_1\) is assumed to lead \(\phi_2\).
  3. Bits (or data) to be stored are written to registers, storage elements, and subsystems on \(\phi_1\) of the clock; that is, write signal WR are ANDed with \(\phi_1\).
  4. Bits on data written into storage elements may be assumed to have settled before the immediately following \(\phi_2\) signal, and \(\phi_2\) signal may be used to refresh stored data where appropriate.
  5. In general, delays through data path, combinational logic, etc, are assumed to be less than the interval between the leading edge of \(\phi_1\) of the clock and the leading edge of the following \(\phi_2\) signal.
  6. Bits on data may be read from storage elements on the next \(\phi_1\) of the clock; that is, read signals RD are ANDed with \(\phi_1\). Obviously, RD and WR are
Page 13
📺 Watch Lecture 3T DRAM Cell: 3T-DRAM Cell Circuit & Read/Write Operation (Flop_n_Adder) ↗

generally mutually exclusive to any one storage element.

  1. A general requirement for system stability is that there must be at least one clocked storage element in series with every closed loop signal path.
Fig: Two-phase non-overlapping clock waveforms

# A three-transistor Dynamic RAM cell

Fig: (a) circuit arrangement
Fig: (b) cmos stick diagram
Vertical poly/metal lines: WR, φ, RD (red = metal, blue = diffusion,
in original colored sketch) with p-type pull-up transistor at top
and n-type pass/pull-down transistors T1,T2,T3 arranged along BUS,
GND lines below.
Page 14
📺 Watch Lecture 3T DRAM Cell: 3T-DRAM Cell Circuit & Read/Write Operation (Flop_n_Adder) ↗

Note WR and RD are coincident with \(\phi_1\).

Action is as follow:

  1. With the RD control line in the low (logic 0) state, then a bit may be read from the bus through T1 by taking WR to the High (logic1) state so that the logic level on the bus is... communicated to the gate capacitance of T2. Then WR is taken Low again
  2. The bit value is then stored for some time by \(C_g\) of T2 while both RD and WR are low.
  3. To read the stored bit it is only necessary to make RD high and the bus will be pulled down to ground through T3 and T2 if a 1 was stored. Otherwise, T2 will be nonconducting and the bus will remain high due to pull-up arrangement.

The concept of the single transistor cell quite simple. It basically consist of a capacitor \(C_m\) which can be charged during write from the read/write line, provided that the row select line on High (logic 1). The state of the \(C_m\) change \(C_m\) can be read subsequently by detecting the state of the change via the same read/write line with row select line High again, and a sense amplifier of a suitable nature can be designed to differentiate between a stored 0 and a stored 1.

# A pseudo-static RAM/register cell

All the storage elements consider have been volatile and thus have a implied need to be periodically refreshed. A common way of meeting this need is to store a bit in two inverter stage in series with feedback, say, on \(\phi_2\) to refresh the data every clock cycle.

* It will be seen that a bit may be written to the cell from the bus by energizing the WR line. From our system timing consideration, we will assume WR to occur in coincidence with \(\phi_1\) of the clock. Thus, the bit is stored on \(C_g\) of... inverter1 and will be reproduced complemented at the output

Page 17
📺 Watch Lecture 6T SRAM Cell Operation: 6T SRAM Cell Read & Write Operation (Getyr_ECE) ↗

of inverter1 and true at the output of inverter2. It will be seen that during every \(\phi_2\) of the clock the stored bit is refreshed through the gated feedback path from the output of inverter2 to the input inverter1. Thus the bit will be held as long as \(\phi_2\) of the clock recurs at intervals less than the decay time of the stored bit. To read the state of the cell it is only necessary to energize the RD line, which is also assumed coincident with \(\phi_1\) of the clock and the bit will be read into the bus.

Page 18
📺 Watch Lecture 6T SRAM Cell Operation: 6T SRAM Cell Read & Write Operation (Getyr_ECE) ↗

Fig: Dynamic and static memory cells

Fig-(a) gives the arrangement for a four-transistor dynamic cell for storing one bit. Each bit is stored on the gate capacitance of two n-type transistor T1 and T2.

Write operation:

Both \(\text{bit}\) and \(\overline{\text{bit}}\) buses are precharged to \(V_{DD}\) (logic 1) in coincidence with \(\phi_1\) of an assumed two phase clock. Precharging is effect via the p transistor T5 and T6 in the diagram. Now (fig-c), the appropriate 'column select' line is activated in coincidence with clock phase \(\phi_2\) and either the bit on bit line is discharged by the logic level present on the I/O bus line, the I/O line acting in this case as a current sink when carrying a logic 0. The 'row select' signal is activated at the same time as 'column select' and the bit line state are 'written in' via T3 and T4 and stored by T1 and T2 as changes on gate capacitances \(C_{g2}\) and \(C_{g1}\) respectively.

Read operation:

Once again both \(\text{bit}\) and \(\overline{\text{bit}}\) lines are precharged to \(V_{DD}\) via T5 and T6 during \(\phi_1\), so that both line will be at logic1. Now if, say, a 1 has been stored T2 will be on and T1 will be off, and thus the bit line will be discharged to \(V_{SS}\) (logic0) through and the stored bit thus reappears on the bit lines.

Finite State Machine (FSM) Design using Verilog

Mealy vs. Moore FSM Models:

State machines are sequential circuits that transition between states based on current inputs and clock edges. The primary classification is based on how outputs are generated:

  • Moore Machine: The outputs are determined only by the current state.
    \[\text{Output}(t) = f(\text{Current State}(t))\]
    *Advantage:* Glitch-free outputs because they transition synchronously with the state register clock edge.
  • Mealy Machine: The outputs are determined by both the current state and the current inputs.
    \[\text{Output}(t) = f(\text{Current State}(t), \text{Input}(t))\]
    *Advantage:* Typically requires fewer states to implement the same logic function; transitions are faster because inputs can change outputs asynchronously within the same cycle.

Structured Verilog Modeling (Two-Process FSM):

In structured Verilog HDL design, FSMs are modeled using two processes (or `always` blocks):

  1. Sequential Process: A clocked block (`always @(posedge clk)`) that updates the state register with the next state value.
  2. Combinational Process: A logic block (`always @(*)`) that computes the next state and the outputs based on inputs and the current state.

Verilog Code Example — Moore FSM (Sequence Detector: 101):

module moore_101_detector (
    input clk, reset, in,
    output reg out
);
    typedef enum [1:0] {S_RESET, S_1, S_10, S_101} state_t;
    state_t state, next_state;

    // 1. Sequential State Register
    always @(posedge clk or posedge reset) begin
        if (reset) state <= S_RESET;
        else       state <= next_state;
    end

    // 2. Combinational Next State Logic
    always @(*) begin
        case (state)
            S_RESET: next_state = (in) ? S_1   : S_RESET;
            S_1:     next_state = (in) ? S_1   : S_10;
            S_10:    next_state = (in) ? S_101 : S_RESET;
            S_101:   next_state = (in) ? S_1   : S_10;
            default: next_state = S_RESET;
        endcase
    end

    // 3. Moore Output (Depends ONLY on state)
    always @(*) begin
        out = (state == S_101);
    end
endmodule

Verilog Code Example — Mealy FSM (Sequence Detector: 101):

module mealy_101_detector (
    input clk, reset, in,
    output reg out
);
    typedef enum [1:0] {S_RESET, S_1, S_10} state_t;
    state_t state, next_state;

    // 1. Sequential State Register
    always @(posedge clk or posedge reset) begin
        if (reset) state <= S_RESET;
        else       state <= next_state;
    end

    // 2. Combinational Next State & Output Logic (Output depends on input too!)
    always @(*) begin
        out = 1'b0;
        case (state)
            S_RESET: begin
                next_state = (in) ? S_1 : S_RESET;
            end
            S_1: begin
                next_state = (in) ? S_1 : S_10;
            end
            S_10: begin
                if (in) begin
                    next_state = S_1;
                    out = 1'b1;
                end else begin
                    next_state = S_RESET;
                end
            end
            default: next_state = S_RESET;
        endcase
    end
endmodule

Memory Elements Design: DRAM, SRAM, and Array Architectures

1. 1-Transistor (1T) Dynamic Memory Cell:

The 1T DRAM cell is the most compact memory structure. It consists of a single access NMOS transistor and a storage capacitor \(C_s\):

  • Write: Word Line (WL) is driven high, turning on the access transistor. Bit Line (BL) drives the storage node to \(V_{DD}\) (logic 1) or GND (logic 0).
  • Read: BL is precharged to \(V_{DD}\)/2. WL is driven high. Charge sharing occurs between \(C_s\) and BL capacitance, causing a small voltage change on BL detected by a sense amplifier.

2. 4-Transistor (4T) Dynamic Memory Cell:

A 4T DRAM cell consists of two access transistors and two cross-coupled storage transistors (without pull-up devices). The charge is stored on the gate capacitances of the cross-coupled pair. Write and read are performed differentially using BL and \(\bar{BL}\), offering faster access compared to 1T DRAM.

3. 32-Transistor Static CMOS Register Word (4-bit):

In standard VLSI education (e.g., Dhaka University syllabus), a 32-transistor static CMOS memory cell refers to a 4-bit register block designed using 4 pseudo-static CMOS memory cells. Each 1-bit cell requires exactly 8 transistors:

  • 2 inverters in a feedback loop (4 transistors).
  • 1 input write transmission gate (2 transistors: PMOS+NMOS).
  • 1 feedback control transmission gate (2 transistors: PMOS+NMOS).
  • Total per bit: 8 transistors. For a 4-bit word register: \(8 \times 4 = 32\) transistors!

4. 132-Bit Static CMOS Memory Array:

A small-scale RAM array storing 132 bits of data. It is typically structured as 12 words of 11 bits each (or 33 words of 4 bits):

  • Row Decoder: Takes address inputs and asserts one of the Word Lines (WL).
  • Column Multiplexer / Decoders: Connects selected Bit Lines to the read/write buffers.
  • Write/Read Buffers: Interface data inputs and outputs with the array columns.
Page S-10 (Supp)
📺 Watch Lecture Sequential Logic & Timing: Two-Phase Clocking & CMOS Latches (Sanjay Vidhyadharan) ↗

Clocked Sequential Circuits: Two-Phase Clocking and Dynamic Shift Registers

1. Two-Phase Non-Overlapping Clocking:

Dynamic sequential designs use two non-overlapping clock phases, \(\phi_1\) and \(\phi_2\), to control transmission paths. The phases are structured such that both are never high simultaneously (\(\phi_1 \cdot \phi_2 = 0\)). This prevents race conditions and charge sharing between adjacent logic stages.

2. Dynamic Shift Register:

Consists of cascaded dynamic CMOS inverter stages separated by pass transistors. Pass transistors of odd stages are gated by \(\phi_1\), while even stages are gated by \(\phi_2\). Data is shifted to the intermediate storage node (gate capacitance) during \(\phi_1\) and transferred to the next output during \(\phi_2\).

3. CMOS Latches and Flip-Flops:

  • Static D Latch: Uses cross-coupled inverters with transmission gates to hold state indefinitely when enabled.
  • Dynamic D Latch: Stores state as charge on the gate capacitance of an inverter, requiring periodic refreshing or active clocking.
2024 2024)6)a [5 Marks] Why single-phase dynamic logic can't be cascaded and how this problem can be solved using four-phase clock?
▼
Sol

1. Why Single-Phase Dynamic Logic Cannot Be Cascaded (The Premature Discharge / Race Problem):

Consider two cascaded single-phase dynamic CMOS stages driven by the same clock \(\phi\):

  • During Precharge (\(\phi = 0\)): The precharge PMOS transistors of both Stage 1 and Stage 2 are ON, precharging both output nodes \(Out_1\) and \(Out_2\) to logic `1` (\(V_{DD}\)).
  • During Evaluation (\(\phi = 1\)): Both stages enter the evaluation phase simultaneously. Suppose the inputs to Stage 1 require \(Out_1\) to discharge from `1` \(\to\) `0`. Because of the finite propagation delay (\(\Delta t\)) of Stage 1's Pull-Down Network (PDN), \(Out_1\) remains temporarily at logic `1` at the start of the evaluate phase.
  • Irreversible Charge Loss: While \(Out_1\) is still transitioning from `1` \(\to\) `0`, Stage 2 (which is also evaluating since \(\phi = 1\)) sees a temporary logic `1` at its input \(Out_1\). This falsely turns ON the NMOS transistor in Stage 2's PDN, prematurely discharging \(Out_2\) to \(0\text{ V}\)!
  • Once \(Out_1\) finally reaches `0`, Stage 2's PDN turns OFF, but because Stage 2's precharge PMOS is OFF during evaluation (\(\phi = 1\)), the lost charge on \(Out_2\) can never be recovered until the next clock cycle, producing an erroneous logic `0` output.

2. How Four-Phase Clocked Logic (\(\phi_1, \phi_2, \phi_3, \phi_4\)) Solves the Cascading Problem:

Four-phase dynamic logic divides each clock period into four non-overlapping/staggered clock phases (\(\phi_1, \phi_2, \phi_3, \phi_4\)) and assigns successive logic gates to staggered clock types so each gate cycles through Precharge \(\to\) Evaluate (Sample) \(\to\) Hold:

  1. Precharge Phase: The output node capacitor \(C_{\text{out}}\) is charged to \(V_{DD}\) while the evaluation path to \(\text{GND}\) is disabled, preventing any input transitions from discharging the node.
  2. Evaluate (Sample) Phase: The precharge transistor turns OFF and the evaluate path turns ON only during the phase when the preceding stage is locked in its Hold state. Because the preceding stage's output is already settled and stable, no false transient `1` can cause premature discharge!
  3. Hold (Isolate) Phases: Both the precharge pull-up and evaluate pull-down paths are turned OFF (via a transmission gate or series clock switch), trapping the valid evaluated charge on \(C_{\text{out}}\) and holding it stable while downstream stages safely evaluate.
2024 2024)6)b [5 Marks] What is parity generator? Explain its principle and also draw the stick diagram of output equation of parity generator with nMOS and CMOS inverter.
▼
Sol

1. Definition & Principle of a Parity Generator:

A Parity Generator is a combinational logic circuit that examines an \(n\)-bit binary data word \(A_{n-1} \dots A_1 A_0\) and generates an extra check bit \(P\) (the parity bit) so that the total number of logic `1`s in the transmitted word (data + parity bit) is always even (Even Parity) or always odd (Odd Parity).

  • Iterative Modulo-2 (XOR) Principle: In structured VLSI design, parity is computed iteratively across identical 1-bit slices where the \(i\)-th cell compares the incoming parity \(P_{i-1}\) from the previous bit with the current data bit \(A_i\) using an Exclusive-OR (XOR) operation:
\[P_i = P_{i-1} \oplus A_i = P_{i-1} \cdot \overline{A}_i + \overline{P}_{i-1} \cdot A_i\]
  • If \(A_i = 0\), the parity bit passes through unchanged (\(P_i = P_{i-1}\)).
  • If \(A_i = 1\), the parity bit is inverted (\(P_i = \overline{P}_{i-1}\)).

2. Implementation Using NMOS & CMOS Inverters + Pass/Transmission Switches:

To implement \(P_i = P_{i-1}\overline{A}_i + \overline{P}_{i-1}A_i\), an NMOS or CMOS inverter first generates the complements \(\overline{A}_i\) and \(\overline{P}_{i-1}\). Then two pass transistors (in NMOS) or two Transmission Gates (in CMOS) controlled by \(A_i\) and \(\overline{A}_i\) multiplex either \(P_{i-1}\) (when \(A_i = 0\)) or \(\overline{P}_{i-1}\) (when \(A_i = 1\)) to the output \(P_i\).

2024 2024)7)c [4 Marks] Derive three transistor dynamic memory cell with help of CMOS stick diagram.
▼
Sol

1. Circuit Structure of the 3-Transistor (3T) Dynamic RAM Cell:

A 3T dynamic memory cell stores a single binary bit as electric charge on the parasitic gate-to-channel capacitance (\(C_g\)) of an internal storage transistor \(T_2\), controlled by two access switches \(T_1\) and \(T_3\):

  • \(T_1\) (Write Access Switch): Controlled by the Write Select line (\(\text{WR}\), active during clock phase \(\phi_1\)). Connects the Data Bus to the storage gate node \(C_g\) of \(T_2\).
  • \(T_2\) (Storage Transistor): Its gate capacitance \(C_g\) holds the stored charge (logic `1` \(\implies V_{Cg} \approx V_{DD}\); logic `0` \(\implies V_{Cg} = 0\text{ V}\)). Its source is connected to \(\text{GND}\) and its drain connects to \(T_3\).
  • \(T_3\) (Read Access Switch): Controlled by the Read Select line (\(\text{RD}\)). Placed in series with \(T_2\) between the Bus/Read line and \(\text{GND}\).

2. Write & Non-Destructive Read Operations:

  • Write Operation (on \(\phi_1\)): The data bit (`0` or `1`) is placed on the Bus and \(\text{WR}\) is asserted high (coincident with \(\phi_1\)). \(T_1\) turns ON and charges or discharges \(C_g\) of \(T_2\). When \(\text{WR}\) goes low, the charge remains trapped on \(C_g\).
  • Read Operation (Precharge on \(\phi_1\), Read on \(\phi_2\)): The bus is precharged to \(V_{DD}\) (logic `1`) during \(\phi_1\). Next, \(\text{RD}\) is asserted high:
    • If a logic `1` is stored on \(C_g\), \(T_2\) is ON; since \(T_3\) is also ON, a series path conducts to \(\text{GND}\), discharging the precharged bus to `0` (complemented read-out).
    • If a logic `0` is stored on \(C_g\), \(T_2\) is OFF; the bus remains precharged at `1`. Read-out is non-destructive because \(C_g\) is isolated by \(T_2\)'s gate oxide!
2021-22 2021-22)5)a [5 Marks] Describe the parity generator basic block diagram with CMOS stick diagram and logic equation in VLSI system.
▼
Sol

1. Structured Parity Generator Architecture & Logic Equation:

A parity generator is a combinational subsystem that computes a parity bit \(P\) indicating whether the number of logic `1`s in an \(n\)-bit input word \((A_{n-1} \dots A_1 A_0)\) is even or odd. In structured VLSI layout, it is implemented as a regular 1D cascade of identical 1-bit XOR cells:

\[P_i = P_{i-1} \oplus A_i = P_{i-1} \cdot \overline{A}_i + \overline{P}_{i-1} \cdot A_i\]

where \(P_{-1}\) is initialized to \(0\) for Even Parity (or \(1\) for Odd Parity). For a 4-bit input word \((A_3, A_2, A_1, A_0)\), the overall output equation is:

\[P = A_0 \oplus A_1 \oplus A_2 \oplus A_3\]

2. Block Diagram & CMOS Stick Diagram of Standard Parity Cell:

  • Each bit cell receives \(A_i\) vertically and passes the running parity \(P_{i-1} \to P_i\) horizontally so cells abut seamlessly along the data path pitch.
  • In CMOS stick layout, \(\overline{A}_i\) and \(\overline{P}_{i-1}\) are generated by local inverters, and four Polysilicon gate lines (\(A_i, \overline{A}_i, P_{i-1}, \overline{P}_{i-1}\)) cross the \(p\)-diffusion and \(n\)-diffusion strips (or control a pair of CMOS Transmission Gates) to form \(P_i = P_{i-1} \oplus A_i\).
2021-22 2021-22)6)c [4 Marks] Derive three transistor dynamic memory cell with help of CMOS stick diagram.
▼
Sol

1. Circuit Structure of the 3-Transistor (3T) Dynamic RAM Cell:

A 3T dynamic memory cell stores a single binary bit as electric charge on the parasitic gate-to-channel capacitance (\(C_g\)) of an internal storage transistor \(T_2\), controlled by two access switches \(T_1\) and \(T_3\):

  • \(T_1\) (Write Access Switch): Controlled by the Write Select line (\(\text{WR}\), active during clock phase \(\phi_1\)). Connects the Data Bus to the storage gate node \(C_g\) of \(T_2\).
  • \(T_2\) (Storage Transistor): Its gate capacitance \(C_g\) holds the stored charge (logic `1` \(\implies V_{Cg} \approx V_{DD}\); logic `0` \(\implies V_{Cg} = 0\text{ V}\)). Its source is connected to \(\text{GND}\) and its drain connects to \(T_3\).
  • \(T_3\) (Read Access Switch): Controlled by the Read Select line (\(\text{RD}\)). Placed in series with \(T_2\) between the Bus/Read line and \(\text{GND}\).

2. Write & Non-Destructive Read Operations:

  • Write Operation (on \(\phi_1\)): The data bit (`0` or `1`) is placed on the Bus and \(\text{WR}\) is asserted high (coincident with \(\phi_1\)). \(T_1\) turns ON and charges or discharges \(C_g\) of \(T_2\). When \(\text{WR}\) goes low, the charge remains trapped on \(C_g\).
  • Read Operation (Precharge on \(\phi_1\), Read on \(\phi_2\)): The bus is precharged to \(V_{DD}\) (logic `1`) during \(\phi_1\). Next, \(\text{RD}\) is asserted high:
    • If a logic `1` is stored on \(C_g\), \(T_2\) is ON; since \(T_3\) is also ON, a series path conducts to \(\text{GND}\), discharging the precharged bus to `0` (complemented read-out).
    • If a logic `0` is stored on \(C_g\), \(T_2\) is OFF; the bus remains precharged at `1`. Read-out is non-destructive because \(C_g\) is isolated by \(T_2\)'s gate oxide!
2021-22 2021-22)7)a [4 Marks] Discuss the properties of sequential circuits.
▼
Sol

Unlike combinational logic circuits (whose outputs depend solely on the present inputs), Sequential Circuits incorporate memory elements in a feedback loop with combinational logic. Their fundamental properties are:

  1. State Memory & History Dependence: Sequential circuits store internal state information (\(Q(t)\)) representing the past history of inputs. Both the next state \(Q(t+1)\) and the present output \(Y(t)\) are functions of the present input \(X(t)\) and the stored state \(Q(t)\):
    \[Q(t+1) = \delta\big(Q(t),\, X(t)\big) \qquad\text{and}\qquad Y(t) = \lambda\big(Q(t),\, X(t)\big)\]
  2. Bistability & Regenerative Feedback: Static sequential storage elements (SR latches, D latches, flip-flops, 6T SRAM) use cross-coupled inverters with positive feedback loop gain \(|A_v| > 1\), providing two stable operating states (`0` and `1`) that regenerate degraded logic levels.
  3. Static vs. Dynamic State Storage:
    • Static Sequential Circuits: Retain their stored state indefinitely as long as DC power (\(V_{DD}\)) is applied, via continuous cross-coupled feedback.
    • Dynamic Sequential Circuits: Store state as charge on parasitic gate capacitances (\(C_g\)); require periodic refreshing before charge leaks away (governed by minimum clock frequency).
  4. Synchronous Clocking & Timing Discipline: In synchronous VLSI sequential systems, state transitions occur only at discrete instants controlled by a clock (e.g., two-phase non-overlapping clocks \(\phi_1\) and \(\phi_2\) where \(\phi_1 \cdot \phi_2 = 0\)) to prevent race conditions, and must satisfy Setup Time (\(t_{\text{su}}\)) and Hold Time (\(t_h\)) constraints.
2021-22 2021-22)7)b [4 Marks] What are the differences between Mealy and Moore type finite state machines.
▼
Sol

Finite State Machines (FSMs) are classified into Moore and Mealy models based on how the output logic is generated:

\[\text{Moore Output: } Y(t) = \lambda\big(\text{State}(t)\big) \qquad\text{vs.}\qquad \text{Mealy Output: } Y(t) = \lambda\big(\text{State}(t),\, \text{Input}(t)\big)\]
Comparison FeatureMoore Finite State MachineMealy Finite State Machine
1. Output DependencyDepends ONLY on the present state \(S(t)\).Depends on BOTH the present state \(S(t)\) and present inputs \(X(t)\).
2. State Diagram NotationOutput is written inside each state bubble (e.g., \(S_i / Y\)).Output is written along each transition arrow (e.g., \(X / Y\)).
3. Number of States RequiredGenerally requires more states (typically \(N + 1\) states for an \(N\)-bit sequence detector).Generally requires fewer states (typically \(N\) states for an \(N\)-bit sequence detector).
4. Output SynchronizationOutputs change synchronously only after an active clock edge updates the state register.Outputs can change asynchronously and immediately whenever an input changes within a clock cycle.
5. Glitch / Hazard ImmunityGlitch-free (safe for driving edge-triggered downstream logic).Susceptible to input glitches propagating directly through the output combinational logic.
6. Response SpeedOutput pulse appears one clock cycle later after input match.Reacts immediately in the same clock cycle when the matching input arrives.
2021-22 2021-22)7)c [6 Marks] A Moore type finite state machine (FSM) receives a sequence of one bit binary numbers such as '00100101000' through a one-bit input port and gives a binary '1' output whenever it detects the sequence '01'. Draw the state transition diagrams of this FSM and design the FSM.
▼
Sol

1. Understanding the Problem:

  • The input \(X\) is one bit per clock cycle, and the output \(Z\) is \(1\) when the last two bits received were "0" then "1".
  • Overlapping sequences are allowed. In 00100101000, the pattern "01" occurs three times.
  • In a Moore machine, the output depends only on the present state, so we need a separate state that means "01 just detected".

2. Defining the States:

StateMeaningOutput \(Z\)
\(S_0\)Initial or reset state: nothing useful seen (last bit was 1, or no input yet)\(0\)
\(S_1\)Last bit received was "0" (first half of the pattern seen)\(0\)
\(S_2\)Last two bits were "01" (pattern detected)\(1\)

3. State Transition Diagram:

Each arc is labelled with the input \(X\) that causes the transition, and each state circle shows its Moore output \(Z\):

  • \(S_0\): On \(0\) goes to \(S_1\). On \(1\) stays in \(S_0\) (the "1" does not start the pattern).
  • \(S_1\): On \(0\) stays in \(S_1\) (the latest "0" could still start the pattern). On \(1\) goes to \(S_2\) (pattern complete).
  • \(S_2\): On \(0\) goes to \(S_1\) (this "0" can start a new overlapping match). On \(1\) goes to \(S_0\).
  • Note: \(S_0\) and \(S_2\) have identical next-state behaviour; they are separate states only because their outputs differ (\(Z=0\) vs. \(Z=1\)), as required by a Moore machine.

4. State Table:

Present StateNext State, \(X = 0\)Next State, \(X = 1\)Output \(Z\)
\(S_0\)\(S_1\)\(S_0\)\(0\)
\(S_1\)\(S_1\)\(S_2\)\(0\)
\(S_2\)\(S_1\)\(S_0\)\(1\)

5. State Assignment:

Three states require \(2\) flip-flops \((Q_1, Q_0)\): \(S_0 = 00\), \(S_1 = 01\), \(S_2 = 10\), while state \(11\) is unused and treated as a don't-care (\(d\)).

6. Excitation Table (D Flip-Flops):

For a D flip-flop, the excitation input equals the next state (\(D_1 = Q_1^+\), \(D_0 = Q_0^+\)):

\(Q_1\)\(Q_0\)\(X\)\(D_1\) (next \(Q_1\))\(D_0\) (next \(Q_0\))Output \(Z\)
000010
001000
010010
011100
100011
101001
110\(d\)\(d\)\(d\)
111\(d\)\(d\)\(d\)

7. Simplification with K-Maps:

  • For \(D_1\): The only \(1\) is at \(Q_1 Q_0 X = 011\). Pairing it with the don't-care at \(111\) yields \(D_1 = Q_0 \cdot X\).
  • For \(D_0\): \(D_0 = 1\) for \(X = 0\) across states \(00, 01, 10\), and the don't-care at \(110\) completes the 4-cell group, yielding \(D_0 = \overline{X}\).
  • For Output \(Z\): \(Z = 1\) for \(Q_1 Q_0 = 10\), and pairing with don't-care \(11\) yields \(Z = Q_1\).
\[D_1 = Q_0 \cdot X, \qquad D_0 = \overline{X}, \qquad Z = Q_1\]

8. Circuit Implementation:

  • One 2-input AND gate takes \(Q_0\) and \(X\) and feeds \(D_1\).
  • One NOT gate (Inverter) takes \(X\) and feeds \(D_0\).
  • Two D flip-flops (\(\text{FF}_1, \text{FF}_0\)) share a common clock (\(\text{CLK}\)), with an asynchronous \(\text{RESET}\) to force initial state \(00\) (\(S_0\)).
  • The Moore output \(Z\) is taken directly from \(Q_1\) with no extra logic gate.

9. Verification with the Sequence 00100101000:

Starting from \(S_0\), the state and Moore output \(Z\) after each input bit are:

Input \(X\)00100101000
State\(S_1\)\(S_1\)\(S_2\)\(S_1\)\(S_1\)\(S_2\)\(S_1\)\(S_2\)\(S_1\)\(S_1\)\(S_1\)
Output \(Z\)00100101000

Output \(Z = 1\) appears exactly three times, right after each "01" in the stream.

10. Remarks:

  • Self-Recovery from Unused State 11: If a power-up glitch places the machine in state \(11\), the next clock cycle drives it to \(D_1 D_0 = 01\) (\(S_1\) when \(X=0\)) or \(10\) (\(S_2\) when \(X=1\)), automatically returning to a valid state within one clock cycle.
  • Moore vs. Mealy State Count: A Mealy detector for "01" requires only \(2\) states, whereas a Moore detector requires \(3\) states because the output depends strictly on the state.

Module 10: Testing & Design for Testability

Combinational testing, sensitized path analysis, stuck-at-fault models, scan design, BIST, and JTAG boundaries.

💡 মনে রাখবে | VLSI Testing Short Techniques
  • Rule of Ten: চিপ লেভেলে টেস্ট করতে যে খরচ হয়, তা board লেভেলে ১০ গুণ এবং system লেভেলে ১০০ গুণ বৃদ্ধি পায়। (Shortcut: যত আগে টেস্ট, তত কম খরচ)।
  • Stuck-At-Faults (SA0/SA1):
    • SA0 (Stuck-At-0): লাইনটি সবসময় 0 হয়ে থাকে। একে টেস্ট করার জন্য সেই লাইনে ইনপুট 1 পাঠাতে হয় এবং আউটপুট চেক করতে হয়।
    • SA1 (Stuck-At-1): লাইনটি সবসময় 1 হয়ে থাকে। একে টেস্ট করার জন্য সেই লাইনে ইনপুট 0 পাঠাতে হয়।
  • Sensitized Path Method: একটি নির্দিষ্ট ফল্টকে আউটপুট পর্যন্ত নিয়ে যাওয়ার জন্য ফল্ট লাইনের পথটিকে সক্রিয় করা এবং অন্য লাইনগুলোকে প্যাসিভ (NAND-এর জন্য 1, NOR-এর জন্য 0) রাখা।
  • BIST (Built-in Self Test): চিপের ভেতরেই টেস্ট প্যাটার্ন জেনারেটর (LFSR) এবং সিগনেচার অ্যানালাইজার থাকে, ফলে বাইরের কোনো টেস্টার মেশিনের প্রয়োজন হয় না।
  • Scan Path Testing Working: Test মুডে চিপের সমস্ত সাধারণ ফ্লিপ-ফ্লপ বা রেজিস্টারকে একটি দীর্ঘ শিফট রেজিস্টার (Scan Chain)-এ রূপান্তরিত করা হয়। এর ফলে চিপের ভেতরের যেকোনো নোডে ডেটা ইনপুট দেওয়া (Scan-In) এবং আউটপুট রিড করা (Scan-Out) সহজ হয়।
  • Boundary Scan (JTAG): এটি board লেভেলে আইসিগুলোর পিন কানেকশন টেস্ট করতে ব্যবহার করা হয়। আইসির ইনপুট-আউটপুট পিনগুলোর সাথে বাউন্ডারি স্ক্যান সেল (BSC) যুক্ত থাকে যা JTAG ইন্টারফেসের মাধ্যমে ডেটা এক্সচেঞ্জ করে।

A relationship, known as 'the rule of ten', tends to apply as far as test cost are concerned.

This rule is concisely put as follows:

If chip test cost = $x, then once the chip is soldered into a p.c. board with other components, test cost = $10x.

Further, once that board is integrated into a system/equipment, then the test cost escalates by a further factor of ten cost=$100x.

Finally, a factor which is often overlooked is that test cost may escalate by a further factor of ten when the equipment is in service in the field. It is thus essential to test the chip level as comprehensively as possible.

Testing combinational logic —

* Sensitized path based testing:

  1. Manifestation: Gate input at the site of an assumed fault, say a 'stuck at' (SA) fault, are specified to generate the opposite value to the assumed SA value (0 for SA1, 1 for SA0)
  2. Propagation: Input of other gates are determined so as to propagate the fault signal along the selected path to the primary output of the circuit. This is done by setting AND/NAND input to '1' and OR/NOR inputs to '0'
  3. Consistency (Justification): This final step finds the
Page 28
📺 Watch Lecture Stuck-At Faults (SA0/SA1): Stuck at Fault in Integrated Circuit (SA0 / SA1) (Engineering Funda) ↗
📺 Watch Lecture Path Sensitization ATPG: ATPG & Path Sensitization Technique Explained (DFT CONCEPT) ↗

primary input patterns to realize all the necessary values. This is done by tracing backward from the gate input to the primary input of the logic.

# Ex-1: Take an SA1 fault on line 1 (L1) in Fig-1

Fig-1: Combinational circuit (SA1 fault on line 1)
A ──1──●(SA1)
         │
B ──2────5(AND)──8───┐
                       10(AND)───────┐
C ──3────6(OR)───7────9(inv)─11──────10(NOR)── F
D ──4───┘
(line numbering: A=1, B=2, C=3, D=4, node5=AND(1,2),
node6=OR(3,4)→7, node8=AND(5,6-ish)/AND gate output,
node9=inverter of 7 →11, node10=AND(5,8)→10 into final NOR with 11 → F)

Manifestation: \(L_1 = 0 \implies A = 0\). In a fault-free situation, output \(F\) changes with \(A\) if \(B, C,\) and \(D\) are fixed. If \(L_1\) is SA1, then \(F = 0\) even if \(A = 0\).

Manifestation

\(L_1 = 0 \implies A = 0\)

propagation
AND: \(L_5 = L_8 = 1\)
NOR: \(L_{11} = 0\)
Consistency:
AND: \(L_5 = 1 \implies L_2 = B = 1\)
\(L_8 = 1 \implies L_7 = 1\)
NOT: \(L_{11} = 0 \implies L_9 = L_7 = 1\)
OR: \(L_7 = 1 \implies B + C + D = 1\)

ch-9 # Testing of VLSI circuits #

Testing is the process of applying a set of input stimuli to a fabricated chip and verifying that the outputs match expected results, in order to detect manufacturing defects.

# Need for Testing —

  1. To detect chips with manufacturing defects (faulty chips) before shipping to customers.
  2. To ensure quality and reliability of the product.
  3. To improve yield by identifying process issues.

# Types of faults —

  1. Stuck-at fault — a signal line is permanently stuck at logic 0 (stuck-at-0) or logic 1 (stuck-at-1), regardless of the input.
  2. Bridging fault — two signal lines that should be independent get unintentionally shorted together.
  3. Open fault — a break in a signal line/interconnect causing it to be disconnected.
  4. Delay fault — the circuit works logically correct but fails to meet timing requirements.
Page 123
📺 Watch Lecture Stuck-At Faults (SA0/SA1): Stuck at Fault in Integrated Circuit (SA0 / SA1) (Engineering Funda) ↗

page 123

# Fault models —

* A fault model is a mathematical/abstract representation used to describe the effect of a physical defect on the logical behavior of a circuit, so that test generation algorithms can be applied.

Stuck-at fault model is the most commonly used fault model due to its simplicity and reasonably good defect coverage.

Fig: Stuck-at fault example — AND gate with input line A stuck-at-0 (s-a-0), fault denoted A/0
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ TECHNICAL SCHEMATIC SPECIFICATION                        │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Block : Stuck-at fault example                           │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ • AND gate with input line A stuck-at-0 (s-a-0), fault   │
│   denoted A/0                                            │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

# Test pattern generation —

* Test pattern generation is the process of finding a set of input vectors that can detect (activate & propagate) the presence of a specific fault to a primary output, where it can be observed.

Page 124
📺 Watch Lecture CMOS Testing & DFT: VLSI Design: CMOS Testing & DFT Fundamentals (Sanjay Vidhyadharan) ↗

page 124

# Testability —

* Testability of a circuit is measured by two properties:

  1. Controllability — the ease of setting a particular node in the circuit to a required logic value (0 or 1) from the primary inputs.
  2. Observability — the ease of observing the logic value of a particular node at the primary outputs.

A circuit with poor controllability/observability is difficult to test and requires a large number of test vectors.

# DFT (Design For Testability) —

* DFT refers to design techniques that are added to a circuit specifically to make it easier and faster to test, by improving controllability and observability of internal nodes.

Page 125
📺 Watch Lecture CMOS Testing & DFT: VLSI Design: CMOS Testing & DFT Fundamentals (Sanjay Vidhyadharan) ↗

page 125

# Scan design (Scan-path testing) —

Scan design is a DFT technique in which all (or most) internal flip-flops of a sequential circuit are replaced with scan flip-flops that can be connected together to form one or more shift registers (scan chains) during test mode.

# Working —

  1. In normal mode, scan flip-flops operate as regular flip-flops.
  2. In test mode, the flip-flops are connected in series (scan chain) and test data is shifted in serially (scan-in), applied to combinational logic, results captured, and then shifted out serially (scan-out).
Fig: Scan flip-flop with mux — normal data input D and scan input SI multiplexed by Test-mode select signal, output Q feeds combinational logic and next scan FF
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ TECHNICAL SCHEMATIC SPECIFICATION                        │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Block : Scan flip-flop with mux                          │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ • normal data input D and scan input SI multiplexed by   │
│   Test-mode select signal, output Q feeds combinational  │
│   logic and next scan FF                                 │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

page 126

# Advantage of Scan design —

  1. Converts sequential circuit testing problem into simpler combinational circuit testing problem.
  2. Provides direct controllability & observability of every flip-flop.
  3. Reduces test generation complexity and improves fault coverage.

# Disadvantage —

  1. Extra area overhead (multiplexer added to every flip-flop).
  2. Extra pins required (scan-in, scan-out, test-mode).
  3. Increased test time due to serial shifting.

# BIST (Built-In Self-Test) —

BIST is a DFT technique in which additional on-chip hardware is included to generate test patterns and analyze test responses internally, without needing external test equipment.

Page 127
📺 Watch Lecture BIST Architecture: BIST - Built In Self Test (Types, Architecture & LFSR) (Engineering Funda) ↗

page 127

# BIST architecture —

Fig: BIST block diagram — TPG (Test Pattern Generator, usually LFSR) → Circuit Under Test (CUT) → Output Response Analyzer (ORA, usually MISR/signature analyzer) → Pass/Fail signal

# Components of BIST —

  1. TPG (Test Pattern Generator) — commonly an LFSR (Linear Feedback Shift Register) generating pseudo-random test patterns.
  2. CUT (Circuit Under Test) — the actual logic being tested.
  3. ORA (Output Response Analyzer) — commonly a MISR (Multiple Input Signature Register) that compresses the output responses into a signature.

* The generated signature is compared with a known good (fault-free) signature to determine pass/fail.

Page 128
📺 Watch Lecture BIST Architecture: BIST - Built In Self Test (Types, Architecture & LFSR) (Engineering Funda) ↗

page 128

# Advantages of BIST —

  1. No need for expensive external Automatic Test Equipment (ATE).
  2. At-speed testing possible (tests circuit at its actual operating frequency).
  3. Can be used for field testing (testing after the chip is deployed).

# Disadvantages of BIST —

  1. Extra hardware overhead (area & power).
  2. Aliasing problem — MISR may compress two different faulty responses into the same signature as the fault-free one, causing an undetected fault.
Page 129
📺 Watch Lecture Boundary Scan & JTAG: Boundary Scan & JTAG Explained (IEEE 1149.1) (Digital VLSI Interview Prep) ↗

page 129

# JTAG / Boundary Scan —

Boundary scan (IEEE 1149.1, JTAG) is a DFT technique in which a scan cell is placed at every I/O pin of a chip, allowing testing of interconnections between chips on a board without physical test probes.

# Applications of Boundary Scan —

  1. Testing board-level interconnections between ICs.
  2. In-circuit programming of devices (e.g. FPGA/flash programming via JTAG).
  3. Debugging — accessing internal signals of a chip during development.

— End of Notes —
